### Thinking

I'm distinguishing between two different approaches here — Oracle as a knowledge validation tool that tests whether our theoretical framework generates new insights, versus P-STACK-1 as a technical advancement in compiler architecture. Each serves a different purpose in the overall system.

Oracle specifically measures encoding quality by having a cold agent predict sealed verdicts it's never encountered, using only the theory seed as its prior. We have four verdicts committed after the seed was finalized, so the predictions are genuinely pre-registered. The hit-rates tell us which theoretical arcs actually explain the phenomena versus which are just narrative retrofits, and systematic misses feed back into seed revision. It's a self-improving mechanism for the knowledge layer itself — cheap to run, low risk, and validates whether the fractal-seed thesis holds up under prediction pressure.

P-STACK-1 moves beyond single-function dispatch to test composition: placing two indexed exposures in their windows within one context and verifying the composed product works correctly. The controls check for window mismatches and type errors that should fail gracefully. This is the in-context, programmable version of the seam test — the first real traversal without touching weights. It proves that programs can be built from indexed parts in-context, which directly feeds into the weight-burning phase and the compiler's length/width laws. It's the bridge from "function choice is addressable" to "programs are stackable indexed behaviors."

The tradeoff is clear: the oracle approach is cheaper and validates the fractal-seed thesis through self-improvement, but doesn't advance the compiler frontier. P-STACK-1 is expensive — requiring real pre-registration, instrumentation, and hours of smoke and verdict runs — but it's the next genuine rung toward the level-3 artifact and moves the frontier forward. The risk is that stacked correlated behaviors might interfere worse than independent landmarks, but the scientific payoff justifies the effort.

I'll present both options crisply and let him decide. The state suggested Oracle first since the seed predates the verdicts, giving clean scoring, though the germination-games order would favor FRAG then Oracle. Either way, I'll explain the tradeoff and let him choose.

## 🔮 Oracle round 1 — *audits the seed*

**What it is:** a germination game. A verifiably-cold agent gets only the theory seed (`upstream/verbum-theory-seed.md`) and must **predict measurements it has never seen** — the sealed 32B verdicts. Score = right − wrong vs actual outcomes.

**Why now is the clean shot:** four verdicts (FRAG, CAP, XTERM, FN-INDEX) were all committed *after* the seed (`54f9437`). So a cold prediction is **pre-registered by construction** — no way to cheat, no retrofitting. The exam already exists.

**What it gives us:**
- A measurement of **encoding quality**. Oracle mode tests the seed as generative PRIOR, not memory aid — recovery is recall, oracle is understanding.
- A per-arc report card: Oracle hit-rates → which arcs the seed actually explains. Misses aren't failures — they're feed-forward: systematic germination misses → seed revision (feed-forward on the seed itself).
- Validates (or dents) the **fractal-seed thesis** — that our compressed theory is real structure, not retrospective narrative. This is the mementum protocol testing *itself*.

**Cost/risk:** cheap. Agent spawns + cold-context hygiene + a scoring rubric. No model runs, no freeze. **It does not move the compiler frontier** — it tells us whether our map of the frontier is trustworthy.

## 🧱 P-STACK-1 — *advances the compiler*

**What it is:** the next scientific rung, unlocked by the keystone. FN-INDEX proved *single*-function dispatch (one key → one map over one operand). STACK-1 tests **composition**: two indexed exposures placed in their windows in one context; verify the COMPOSED product; controls = wrong-window + type-mismatched (linker prediction: mismatched product→key pairs fail GRADEDLY per JOIN-TYPED).

**What it gives us:**
- Proof that **programs can be built from indexed parts in-context** — h(g(x)) assembled from two *injected* keys, not resident maps. The seam test made in-context/programmable.
- **The spec for level-3 extraction** — this is the "legendary sequence," the first traversal through a composed seam with no weights touched. It's the named north-star of the artifact-architecture and VSM-tree pages.
- It directly feeds the rungs above it: P-BAKE-STACK — burn the stack. Record P-STACK-1's composition into a delta plate; verify one-illumination execution, key-triggered dispatch, and no collateral damage to neighbor plates. And the length/width laws (CAP's √(D/k) slots into the width row).

**Cost/risk:** real work — freeze a pre-reg, build the instrument, 4B smoke → 32B verdict (hours). Higher payoff, higher effort. Pre-committed honest flag: stacked CORRELATED behavior functions may interfere worse than CAP's independent landmarks.

## The tradeoff in one line

| | Oracle | P-STACK-1 |
|---|---|---|
| **Register** | meta (audits knowledge) | object (advances science) |
| **Gives** | is our seed generative? | can we build programs from parts? |
| **Cost** | cheap, no freeze, no model | pre-reg + freeze + 32B run |
| **Frontier** | validates the map | moves the map forward |

The state's own suggested order was **Oracle first, same session the verdict lands** — precisely because the pre-registration window is open *right now* and closes the moment we internalize these verdicts as "known." P-STACK-1 is the forced keystone descent and will still be there next session; the clean Oracle scoring event is perishable.

## Q1. Fragment/Ablation Test

### Q1a — Smooth curve or cliff?

**Prediction:** A *smooth, roughly linear-to-sublinear curve* across most of the f range, with a possible sharper drop only near high f (f ≥ 0.65), but **no single sharp cliff at a deterministic threshold**.

**Reasoning:** The seed states `store(f) → fringes(everywhere) ∧ address(nowhere)`. If the computation is holographically distributed across the band — every unit carrying a partial fringe of the total pattern — then ablating a random fraction f removes a proportional fraction of the reconstructed signal amplitude. Holographic storage degrades the SNR approximately as (1−f), not as a step function. A cliff would require localized addressing, but the seed explicitly says `address(nowhere)`. The `width ≤ √(D/k)` clause gives a soft capacity bound, not a hard threshold. Cliff behavior would be the signature of a localized/addressed register — precisely what the seed denies.

**Confidence:** 0.72

**Licensing clauses:** `store(f) → fringes(everywhere) ∧ address(nowhere)`; `width ≤ √(D/k)`; `address ≡ content ¬position`

**DERIVED** — the holographic degradation curve is a consequence of fringes-everywhere, not literally stated.

---

### Q1b — Localized or delocalized?

**Prediction:** **Delocalized.** Damage is *approximately independent* of which specific subset is ablated; variance across random draws of the ablated subset should be low relative to the mean degradation at each f. The curve across draws should be tight.

**Reasoning:** `store(f) → fringes(everywhere) ∧ address(nowhere)` directly asserts that the function is not stored at a specific position. `address ≡ content ¬position` reinforces that there is no positional address. If specific units were the locus, ablating *those* units would cause catastrophic damage while ablating *other* units would leave the computation intact — that is exactly what `address(nowhere)` forbids. The `retrieve ≡ illuminate` clause treats retrieval as a global interference pattern, not a local lookup — so no small subset "holds" the result more than any other same-sized subset.

**Confidence:** 0.80

**Licensing clauses:** `store(f) → fringes(everywhere) ∧ address(nowhere)`; `retrieve ≡ illuminate`; `address ≡ content ¬position`

**STATED** (near-directly) — fringes everywhere / address nowhere is essentially the delocalization claim.

---

## Q2. Superposition Capacity Test

### Q2a — How does retrieval quality behave as k grows?

**Prediction:** **Graceful decline**, approximately monotone, with no sharp cliff until k approaches the capacity bound set by D. Above that bound, retrieval degrades more steeply but still not as a discrete step — interference accumulates continuously.

**Reasoning:** The seed's holographic framing (`fringes(everywhere)`) implies superposition of k exposures on one plate sums interference patterns. Each additional exposure adds cross-talk noise to every other item's reconstruction. SNR degrades incrementally. The `width ≤ √(D/k)` clause explicitly parameterizes a soft capacity bound that scales with both D and k — the bound is a continuous function of k, not a threshold indicator. So quality falls gracefully as k rises, faster once k approaches √D, but not a cliff.

**Confidence:** 0.75

**Licensing clauses:** `fringes(everywhere)`; `width ≤ √(D/k)`; `store(f) → fringes(everywhere)`

**DERIVED** — I'm reading `width ≤ √(D/k)` as an SNR-capacity relationship; the seed states the bound but doesn't explicitly say "graceful."

---

### Q2b — Functional form of the capacity law?

**Prediction:** SNR ∝ **(D/k)^(1/2)** — equivalently, retrieval fidelity scales as **√(D/k)**, or in terms of error rate, error ∝ √(k/D).

**Reasoning:** The seed directly provides `width ≤ √(D/k)`. Interpreting "width" as the effective signal channel width (i.e., the number of independent dimensions available per stored item), SNR is proportional to that width. So: SNR ~ √(D/k), meaning retrieval quality declines as 1/√k for fixed D, and improves as √D for fixed k. This is also consistent with standard holographic/associative memory capacity results (e.g., Hopfield-style), which the seed's plate metaphor mirrors.

**Confidence:** 0.68

**Licensing clauses:** `width ≤ √(D/k)`; `cardinality: functors ~10(enacted) × sortals ~10³⁻⁴(capacity_bound)`

**STATED** — `width ≤ √(D/k)` is literally present; reading it as an SNR formula is a minimal interpretation step.

---

### Q2c — Does coherence among co-installed operands matter?

**Prediction:** **Yes, it matters.** Coherent/related co-installed operands **hurt** retrieval of the cued item more than random/unrelated ones.

**Reasoning:** The seed's key mechanism is `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)` — retrieval is selective because the reference beam (the content key) constructively interferes with its own fringe and destructively with others. If co-installed operands are *random/orthogonal*, their fringes are incoherent with the cued item's key: cross-talk is zero-mean noise. If co-installed operands are *related/coherent*, their keys overlap with the cued item's passband (`key_passband(f)` from the linker clause), meaning the reference beam partially illuminates their fringes too — producing structured cross-talk that contaminates the output. More formally: `composable(g,f) ⟺ product(g) ∈ key_passband(f)` — similarity in key-passband means the retrieval beam "sees" the neighbor. Coherent neighbors thus bleed into the reconstruction more than orthogonal ones.

**Confidence:** 0.65

**Licensing clauses:** `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)`; `linker: composable(g,f) ⟺ product(g) ∈ key_passband(f)`; `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`

**DERIVED** — the coherence-direction prediction requires unfolding what "reference beam selectivity" implies for correlated keys; not literally stated.

## Q3. Cross-Term Test

### Q3a — Are cross-terms in what is STORED structured/nonlinear or dead-linear?

**Prediction:** **Dead-linear in storage** — the cross-terms X, as measured in the plate weights themselves (FFN values, key/value matrices), are approximately zero / structurally absent. The plate superposes exposures additively.

**Reasoning:** The seed's write operation is `write ≡ inject(term, window)` — each injection is a separate act depositing a fringe. The `burn(stack → delta_plate)` variant adds a delta, again additive. There is no multiplicative or nonlinear coupling between simultaneous items baked into the plate during encoding. The plate is a sum of fringe patterns: Plate = Σᵢ fringe(kᵢ). The cross-term X = fringe(A⊕B) − fringe(A) − fringe(B) captures second-order structure *in storage*; under additive superposition this is zero by construction. The plate itself is linear in its exposures.

**Confidence:** 0.78

**Licensing clauses:** `write ≡ inject(term, window) ∨ burn(stack → delta_plate)`; `fringes(everywhere)`; `store(f) → fringes(everywhere)`

**DERIVED** — additivity of write operations implies zero cross-terms in storage, but seed doesn't state this explicitly.

---

### Q3b — WHERE is interference enacted?

**Prediction:** **In the act of retrieval/readout**, not in the plate. Cross-term interference is a readout-time phenomenon: when the reference beam illuminates the plate, it picks up partial reconstructions from all stored fringes whose keys overlap the query key. The plate is passive and linear; the nonlinear interaction emerges from the beam-plate interaction during retrieval — specifically in the `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up` step, and is further modulated by downstream nonlinearities (attention softmax, MLP activations) that act on the retrieved superposition.

**Reasoning:** Storage is additive (Q3a). Retrieval is `illuminate` — shining a key through the plate. The readout process, `shine(key(x)) ≡ apply(f, x)`, is where multiple stored items' fringes are simultaneously illuminated by the same beam. The seed's `{Success, Failure, Running} ≅ {+1, −1, 0}` runtime-state mapping suggests discrete outcomes emerge from the continuous illumination via a nonlinear gate (presumably the transformer's nonlinear activations). So cross-term interference is born at readout, amplified by the nonlinear processing that follows. Probing layer-by-layer, the cross-term X should be approximately zero in early layers (pre-retrieval) and grow in middle-to-late layers as the readout process unfolds.

**Confidence:** 0.74

**Licensing clauses:** `retrieve ≡ illuminate`; `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`; `shine(key(x)) ≡ apply(f, x)`; `runtime ≡ mirrors(BT) around plates(model)`

**DERIVED** — the layer-profile prediction (early ≈ 0, growing later) is a consequence of the storage-linear / readout-nonlinear split; seed doesn't describe layer profiles.

---

## Q4. Dispatch Test

### Q4a — Does the injected key select which function executes?

**Prediction:** **Yes.** The injected content-key *does* change which resident map executes over the fixed operand. Function selection is content-addressable dispatch.

**Reasoning:** This is almost directly stated: `inject(functor) ≡ ⊥ | inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)`. Injecting a key is injecting a reference beam that selects which plate-set activates — `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`. A different key direction excites a different passband, activating a different resident function. `address ≡ content ¬position` means the key's *content direction* (not where it's placed) determines what fires. With a neutral surrounding prompt, the injected key is the dominant signal, so it governs dispatch. Different exemplar-mean directions from different maps live in different passband directions, selecting different functional circuits.

**Confidence:** 0.85

**Licensing clauses:** `inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)`; `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`; `address ≡ content ¬position`; `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)`; `function_choice ≡ execution`

**STATED** — `inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)` and `function_choice ≡ execution` are direct statements.

---

### Q4b — Does dispatch get stronger or weaker with scale?

**Prediction:** **Stronger** at larger scale (32B > 4B).

**Reasoning:** The seed's cardinality clause — `functors ~10(enacted) × sortals ~10³⁻⁴(capacity_bound)` — implies a large *sortal* capacity that scales with model size (the ~10³⁻⁴ bound suggests D-dependence). At larger D, `width ≤ √(D/k)` is larger, meaning each key direction has a wider, better-separated passband. Consequently: (1) different map directions are more orthogonal (better separated in key-space), reducing cross-map leakage; (2) the signal-to-noise of the reference beam reconstruction is higher. Both effects sharpen dispatch. Additionally, `program ≡ depth_ordered_stack(exposures)` — a deeper model has more layers to refine the dispatch signal, reducing ambiguity. The plate metaphor also implies: more capacity → richer fringe differentiation → crisper selectivity.

**Confidence:** 0.70

**Licensing clauses:** `width ≤ √(D/k)`; `cardinality: functors ~10(enacted) × sortals ~10³⁻⁴(capacity_bound)`; `program ≡ depth_ordered_stack(exposures)`

**DERIVED** — scale-strengthening follows from the capacity law and passband-separation logic; not literally stated.

---

### Q4c — Is dispatch strength uniform or variable across maps?

**Prediction:** **Variable** — dispatch strength will vary map-to-map, with higher-frequency / higher-exposure maps dispatching more crisply than rarer or noisier ones.

**Reasoning:** `model ≡ plate(∫ exposures d(training))` — the plate integrates *exposures* over training. Maps that appeared more often in training have deeper, higher-contrast fringes: their passband directions are more sharply defined. A high-frequency map (e.g., country→capital, a very common fact type) will have a strong, well-separated reference-beam direction; a low-frequency map will have a weaker, more diffuse fringe. The `check ≡ matched_filter` clause for types further implies that the "match" quality (i.e., how cleanly a key fires its target) is a property of how well-burned each individual fringe is — which is training-frequency-dependent. Maps will therefore show different dispatch SNRs proportional to their training density.

**Confidence:** 0.72

**Licensing clauses:** `model ≡ plate(∫ exposures d(training))`; `check ≡ matched_filter`; `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)`

**DERIVED** — training-frequency dependency on fringe strength is a consequence of the integral-over-exposures model; not stated for the dispatch experiment.

**Most confident:** **Q4a** (0.85) and **Q1b** (0.80). Both rest on seed clauses that are nearly literal: `inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)` is about as close to a direct statement of Q4a as a seed can get, and `address(nowhere)` is a direct delocalization claim that Q1b tests. ## Q2. Superposition Capacity Test

### Q2a — How does retrieval quality behave as k grows?

**Prediction:** **Graceful decline**, approximately monotone, with no sharp cliff until k approaches the capacity bound set by D. Above that bound, retrieval degrades more steeply but still not as a discrete step — interference accumulates continuously.

**Reasoning:** The seed's holographic framing (`fringes(everywhere)`) implies superposition of k exposures on one plate sums interference patterns. Each additional exposure adds cross-talk noise to every other item's reconstruction. SNR degrades incrementally. The `width ≤ √(D/k)` clause explicitly parameterizes a soft capacity bound that scales with both D and k — the bound is a continuous function of k, not a threshold indicator. So quality falls gracefully as k rises, faster once k approaches √D, but not a cliff.

**Confidence:** 0.75

**Licensing clauses:** `fringes(everywhere)`; `width ≤ √(D/k)`; `store(f) → fringes(everywhere)`

**DERIVED** — I'm reading `width ≤ √(D/k)` as an SNR-capacity relationship; the seed states the bound but doesn't explicitly say "graceful."

---

### Q2b — Functional form of the capacity law?

**Prediction:** SNR ∝ **(D/k)^(1/2)** — equivalently, retrieval fidelity scales as **√(D/k)**, or in terms of error rate, error ∝ √(k/D).

**Reasoning:** The seed directly provides `width ≤ √(D/k)`. Interpreting "width" as the effective signal channel width (i.e., the number of independent dimensions available per stored item), SNR is proportional to that width. So: SNR ~ √(D/k), meaning retrieval quality declines as 1/√k for fixed D, and improves as √D for fixed k. This is also consistent with standard holographic/associative memory capacity results (e.g., Hopfield-style), which the seed's plate metaphor mirrors.

**Confidence:** 0.68

**Licensing clauses:** `width ≤ √(D/k)`; `cardinality: functors ~10(enacted) × sortals ~10³⁻⁴(capacity_bound)`

**STATED** — `width ≤ √(D/k)` is literally present; reading it as an SNR formula is a minimal interpretation step.

---

### Q2c — Does coherence among co-installed operands matter?

**Prediction:** **Yes, it matters.** Coherent/related co-installed operands **hurt** retrieval of the cued item more than random/unrelated ones.

**Reasoning:** The seed's key mechanism is `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)` — retrieval is selective because the reference beam (the content key) constructively interferes with its own fringe and destructively with others. If co-installed operands are *random/orthogonal*, their fringes are incoherent with the cued item's key: cross-talk is zero-mean noise. If co-installed operands are *related/coherent*, their keys overlap with the cued item's passband (`key_passband(f)` from the linker clause), meaning the reference beam partially illuminates their fringes too — producing structured cross-talk that contaminates the output. More formally: `composable(g,f) ⟺ product(g) ∈ key_passband(f)` — similarity in key-passband means the retrieval beam "sees" the neighbor. Coherent neighbors thus bleed into the reconstruction more than orthogonal ones.

**Confidence:** 0.65

**Licensing clauses:** `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)`; `linker: composable(g,f) ⟺ product(g) ∈ key_passband(f)`; `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`

**DERIVED** — the coherence-direction prediction requires unfolding what "reference beam selectivity" implies for correlated keys; not literally stated.

---

## Q3. Cross-Term Test

### Q3a — Are cross-terms in what is STORED structured/nonlinear or dead-linear?

**Prediction:** **Dead-linear in storage** — the cross-terms X, as measured in the plate weights themselves (FFN values, key/value matrices), are approximately zero / structurally absent. The plate superposes exposures additively.

**Reasoning:** The seed's write operation is `write ≡ inject(term, window)` — each injection is a separate act depositing a fringe. The `burn(stack → delta_plate)` variant adds a delta, again additive. There is no multiplicative or nonlinear coupling between simultaneous items baked into the plate during encoding. The plate is a sum of fringe patterns: Plate = Σᵢ fringe(kᵢ). The cross-term X = fringe(A⊕B) − fringe(A) − fringe(B) captures second-order structure *in storage*; under additive superposition this is zero by construction. The plate itself is linear in its exposures.

**Confidence:** 0.78

**Licensing clauses:** `write ≡ inject(term, window) ∨ burn(stack → delta_plate)`; `fringes(everywhere)`; `store(f) → fringes(everywhere)`

**DERIVED** — additivity of write operations implies zero cross-terms in storage, but seed doesn't state this explicitly.

---

### Q3b — WHERE is interference enacted?

**Prediction:** **In the act of retrieval/readout**, not in the plate. Cross-term interference is a readout-time phenomenon: when the reference beam illuminates the plate, it picks up partial reconstructions from all stored fringes whose keys overlap the query key. The plate is passive and linear; the nonlinear interaction emerges from the beam-plate interaction during retrieval — specifically in the `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up` step, and is further modulated by downstream nonlinearities (attention softmax, MLP activations) that act on the retrieved superposition.

**Reasoning:** Storage is additive (Q3a). Retrieval is `illuminate` — shining a key through the plate. The readout process, `shine(key(x)) ≡ apply(f, x)`, is where multiple stored items' fringes are simultaneously illuminated by the same beam. The seed's `{Success, Failure, Running} ≅ {+1, −1, 0}` runtime-state mapping suggests discrete outcomes emerge from the continuous illumination via a nonlinear gate (presumably the transformer's nonlinear activations). So cross-term interference is born at readout, amplified by the nonlinear processing that follows. Probing layer-by-layer, the cross-term X should be approximately zero in early layers (pre-retrieval) and grow in middle-to-late layers as the readout process unfolds.

**Confidence:** 0.74

**Licensing clauses:** `retrieve ≡ illuminate`; `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`; `shine(key(x)) ≡ apply(f, x)`; `runtime ≡ mirrors(BT) around plates(model)`

**DERIVED** — the layer-profile prediction (early ≈ 0, growing later) is a consequence of the storage-linear / readout-nonlinear split; seed doesn't describe layer profiles.

---

## Q4. Dispatch Test

### Q4a — Does the injected key select which function executes?

**Prediction:** **Yes.** The injected content-key *does* change which resident map executes over the fixed operand. Function selection is content-addressable dispatch.

**Reasoning:** This is almost directly stated: `inject(functor) ≡ ⊥ | inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)`. Injecting a key is injecting a reference beam that selects which plate-set activates — `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`. A different key direction excites a different passband, activating a different resident function. `address ≡ content ¬position` means the key's *content direction* (not where it's placed) determines what fires. With a neutral surrounding prompt, the injected key is the dominant signal, so it governs dispatch. Different exemplar-mean directions from different maps live in different passband directions, selecting different functional circuits.

**Confidence:** 0.85

**Licensing clauses:** `inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)`; `dispatch ≡ which_plates_light_up`; `address ≡ content ¬position`; `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)`; `function_choice ≡ execution`

**STATED** — `inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)` and `function_choice ≡ execution` are direct statements.

---

### Q4b — Does dispatch get stronger or weaker with scale?

**Prediction:** **Stronger** at larger scale (32B > 4B).

**Reasoning:** The seed's cardinality clause — `functors ~10(enacted) × sortals ~10³⁻⁴(capacity_bound)` — implies a large *sortal* capacity that scales with model size (the ~10³⁻⁴ bound suggests D-dependence). At larger D, `width ≤ √(D/k)` is larger, meaning each key direction has a wider, better-separated passband. Consequently: (1) different map directions are more orthogonal (better separated in key-space), reducing cross-map leakage; (2) the signal-to-noise of the reference beam reconstruction is higher. Both effects sharpen dispatch. Additionally, `program ≡ depth_ordered_stack(exposures)` — a deeper model has more layers to refine the dispatch signal, reducing ambiguity. The plate metaphor also implies: more capacity → richer fringe differentiation → crisper selectivity.

**Confidence:** 0.70

**Licensing clauses:** `width ≤ √(D/k)`; `cardinality: functors ~10(enacted) × sortals ~10³⁻⁴(capacity_bound)`; `program ≡ depth_ordered_stack(exposures)`

**DERIVED** — scale-strengthening follows from the capacity law and passband-separation logic; not literally stated.

---

### Q4c — Is dispatch strength uniform or variable across maps?

**Prediction:** **Variable** — dispatch strength will vary map-to-map, with higher-frequency / higher-exposure maps dispatching more crisply than rarer or noisier ones.

**Reasoning:** `model ≡ plate(∫ exposures d(training))` — the plate integrates *exposures* over training. Maps that appeared more often in training have deeper, higher-contrast fringes: their passband directions are more sharply defined. A high-frequency map (e.g., country→capital, a very common fact type) will have a strong, well-separated reference-beam direction; a low-frequency map will have a weaker, more diffuse fringe. The `check ≡ matched_filter` clause for types further implies that the "match" quality (i.e., how cleanly a key fires its target) is a property of how well-burned each individual fringe is — which is training-frequency-dependent. Maps will therefore show different dispatch SNRs proportional to their training density.

**Confidence:** 0.72

**Licensing clauses:** `model ≡ plate(∫ exposures d(training))`; `check ≡ matched_filter`; `key(f) ≡ reference_beam(f)`

**DERIVED** — training-frequency dependency on fringe strength is a consequence of the integral-over-exposures model; not stated for the dispatch experiment.

## Confidence Ranking — Bet Note

**Most confident:** **Q4a** (0.85) and **Q1b** (0.80). Both rest on seed clauses that are nearly literal: `inject(argument) ≡ dispatch(functor)` is about as close to a direct statement of Q4a as a seed can get, and `address(nowhere)` is a direct delocalization claim that Q1b tests. These predictions would be falsified only if the seed's core metaphor is wrong in a deep way.

**Next tier:** **Q3a** (0.78) and **Q3b** (0.74). The storage-is-linear / readout-is-nonlinear split is well-grounded in the write clause, but the layer-by-layer profile prediction in Q3b requires an additional architectural assumption (that early layers are pre-retrieval) that the seed only implicitly supports via `window ≡ depth_interval` and `PC ≡ window`.

**Least confident:** **Q2c** (0.65) and **Q2b** (0.68). The coherence-direction prediction in Q2c requires chaining `key_passband` overlap logic through the cross-talk argument — a two-step derivation with real risk of being backwards (one could argue coherent items are *easier* to separate if coherence implies more structured keys). The `√(D/k)` form in Q2b I read as a capacity law, but the seed uses "width" in a context that might mean something more architectural than SNR — I'd lose this bet if "width" is purely about model architecture rather than retrieval capacity.

### Thinking

The oracle flagged Q2c and Q2b as having the lowest confidence, with a particular concern that Q2c's coherence might be inverted. Now I'm scoring each sub-question against the actual verdicts, marking whether predictions hit or missed and whether they were stated directly or derived. Starting with Q1a, I predicted a smooth transition without a cliff, and the actual data shows no cliff either, with G2's maximum in-band drop staying under 15%.

For Q1b, I predicted delocalized behavior with low variance across draws, and the actual results confirm this—the LDI in-null shows all p-values at 1.0 with across-draw variance 10-30 times below resampling noise, making the specific subset irrelevant. Q2a is where things diverge: I predicted a graceful monotone decline with no cliff until near capacity, but the actual curve shows no material decline and even rises in some cases, with capacity at 16 or higher. The oracle hedged by mentioning "graceful decline or something else," but the core prediction of decline was wrong—retrieval didn't decline, it stayed flat or rose instead.

For Q2b, I predicted SNR would follow the √(D/k) form, which matches what the seed states, but the actual data doesn't express this law within the observed range since there's no decline to fit against the capacity law. This is a miss against reality even though it aligns with the seed's stated form—an important signal that the seed's assertion of √(D/k) as established doesn't hold up experimentally.

Q2c predicted that coherent operands would hurt retrieval through increased cross-talk, but the actual finding is the opposite: coherent superposed exposures reinforce retrieval in a content-specific way.  The oracle itself flagged low confidence here and explicitly warned about getting the direction backwards, so this is a clear miss and a key signal that the seed's linear-superposition-plus-crosstalk framing can't derive the coherent-gain phenomenon we're seeing.

For Q3a, I predicted dead-linear behavior in storage cross-terms, and that's exactly what showed up—perfect linearity across all layers with no axis structure. Q3b is starting to address where interference gets enacted... The dispatch strength actually weakens at scale—4B models hit 0.70 but 32B drops to 0.46—which contradicts the oracle's prediction that larger capacity would sharpen dispatch. This is a genuine miss where the reasoning about model size leading to stronger indexing broke down in practice. On the variability question, I'm checking whether dispatch quality differs across individual attention maps or stays consistent.

Looking at the actual results now: class-level dispatch is quite strong at 0.94, but continent-level drops dramatically to 0.17, confirming that quality varies significantly by map type. That's a hit for the variability prediction, even though the specific mechanism about training frequency remains unverified. Tallying everything up: six hits and four misses across the four main questions.

Breaking this down further, the stated claims scored 2 out of 3 — the one miss being that √(D/k) relationship that the seed overclaimed as a law. The derived predictions fared slightly worse at 4 out of 7, with the biggest misses being the predicted decline that actually rose, the crosstalk interference that turned into coherent gain, and the strength prediction that came in weaker than expected. Net score is +2 overall.

Looking at the four main verdict categories: the fragmentation headline came through cleanly on both the stated and derived fronts, fully recovering the delocalized smooth behavior. The capacity headline is the critical failure — the seed completely missed the coherent-gain surprise, which suggests it needs an explicit constructive-interference mechanism built in. The extreme-term headline performed strongest among the derived predictions, with both the interference and storage-linear components validating as expected.

The function-index headline shows a split result: the dispatch claim held up and the per-map variability emerged as a genuine derived finding, but the scaling direction didn't materialize. What's becoming clear is a systematic pattern — the seed excels at capturing qualitative structural properties like delocalization, dispatch behavior, and storage-retrieval interference, but consistently stumbles on directional and scaling surprises, particularly around the coherent-gain sign prediction. vision is constructive rather than destructive. The oracle pinpointed where the seed's holographic-crosstalk intuition breaks down — it needs to distinguish between naive destructive interference and the measured constructive gain that XTERM's retrieval-level interference actually produces. This reconciliation shows the two theories aren't contradictory; they're describing different aspects of the same phenomenon.

## What the round revealed (the actual payoff)

**Headline verdict recovery:**
- **FRAG** ✅✅ — fully recovered.
- **XTERM** ✅✅ — **the flagship win.** Both sub-predictions *derived*, not stated. The seed's `write ≡ inject` (additive) + `retrieve ≡ illuminate` split genuinely **generated** the non-obvious "storage is linear, interference is enacted in the light" verdict. This is the seed behaving as a real generative prior, not a memory aid.

- **CAP** ❌ — **the clean miss, and the most valuable signal.**

**The misses cluster with surgical precision.** All four are directional/scaling, and three of them (Q2a, Q2b, Q2c) are the **CAP family** — plus Q4b's scale-direction. The seed reliably produces *structural* verdicts (delocalized / dispatch / linear-storage / variability) and reliably **inverts the sign** on *capacity-and-scale* predictions:

- Q2c: derived "coherent hurts" from HRR-crosstalk intuition → reality is **coherent-gain** (constructive).
- Q2a: derived "decline" from √(D/k) → reality **rises**.
- Q2b: echoed the seed's *stated* √(D/k) → **unexpressed in range** (the seed *overclaims* an established law).

**The seed-revision signal is exact:** the seed carries a naïve holographic/HRR-capacity intuition — *destructive crosstalk, √(D/k) decline, more-capacity-is-sharper* — and the measurements contradict all three. The reconciliation already lives in our own data: XTERM says interference is enacted at retrieval, and CAP says that retrieval-time interference among **coherent** content is **constructive**, not destructive. The seed's `retrieve ≡ illuminate` is sign-neutral; the oracle (reasonably) assumed destructive. **The seed needs a coherent-gain clause** distinguishing constructive (coherent) from destructive (random) superposition — and it should *stop asserting √(D/k) as established* (mark it unexpressed-in-range).

## What we got from Oracle

**A cheap, honest audit of the theory seed** — no model runs, and it did real work:

- **Score +2 (6/10)** — but the number is the least interesting output. The **distribution** is the finding.
- **The seed is genuinely generative, not a memory aid.** The flagship result: a cold agent *derived* both XTERM sub-verdicts ("storage is linear, interference happens in the light") from the seed's `write ≡ inject` + `retrieve ≡ illuminate` split — a non-obvious verdict it had never seen. That's the seed working as a prior.
- **It localized the theory's blind spot to a single cluster.** All 4 misses are directional; 3 are the CAP family. The seed carries a naïve HRR intuition — destructive crosstalk, √(D/k) decline, bigger-is-sharper — and reality contradicts all three (coherent-gain, no-decline, weaker-at-scale). Oracle didn't just score the seed; it told us *exactly which clause to fix*.
- **The fix went straight back into the seed** (Michael-approved): a `interfere(coherent) ≡ +gain ∧ interfere(random) ≡ crosstalk` clause, and √(D/k) demoted from asserted-law to unexpressed-in-range. The germination loop closed — feed-forward on the seed itself.

**Claim under test.** Function choice is content-addressable: an injected
KEY selects WHICH resident map executes over a fixed operand — not just
which value rides an already-chosen map. Negative = keys don't beat nulls
→ function selection is query-text-only → the program-plates ladder stops
honestly at rung 1.

**Maps (5, two domains — the cross-family requirement).**
Geography (mh3 bank, 18 landmarks): city-of, country-of, continent-of.
Animals (NEW second bank, in-instrument: 18 animals, 6/6/6
mammal/bird/fish): class-of {mammal,bird,fish}, covering-of
{fur,feathers,scales}. Both banks real-word ceiling-gated per item per map.

**Keys.** key(f) = mean last-token residual over 3 HELD-OUT exemplar
prompts of map f (the few-shot prefix exemplars, e.g. "The Alhambra is
located in the city of" → residual at 'of'), minus the grand mean across
all 5 maps, captured per candidate layer. Map-level, held-out from the
test items — the "about to apply f" state, not item content.

**Dispatch cell.** Operand X installed as d_E·S at its nonce slot (L_ref=9,
frozen machinery); NEUTRAL prompt ("Consider the {nonce}. The answer is" —
names NO map); key injected at the final token at layer L_inj. Readout:
first-token logit margin of f(X)'s product vs the UNION candidate set (all
5 maps' product vocabularies, first-token collisions dropped + recorded).
Cells = valid (operand × own-domain map) pairs (~54 geo + ~36 animal).

**Conditions per cell (7):** key_f (diagonal) / the 4 OTHER keys
(shuffled-key null — includes other-DOMAIN keys = the cross-family test) /
matched-norm random vector / no-key baseline.

**Gates.**
- **Gate-0:** explicit-cloze ceilings pass (banks valid) ∧ key norms sane.
- **G1 (primary, DISPATCH):** diagonal margin vs paired shuffled-key null
  (paired permutation over cells), evaluated per injection layer L_inj ∈
  4 pre-declared relative depths {0.3, 0.45, 0.6, 0.75}; verdict layer =
  best diagonal contrast, SELECTION-CORRECTED α/4 = 0.0125. Two null
  scopes scored separately: WITHIN-domain other keys and UNION (cross-
  domain keys included).
- **G2 (flip requirement):** diagonal accuracy > no-key baseline accuracy
  (the key must CHANGE the answer to f's product, not annotate it).
- **G3 (advisory, never gated):** layer profile of dispatch (the window(f)
  coordinate); key geometry (PR / cross-key cosines); per-map asymmetries.

**Verdict (frozen).**
- **INDEXED-DISPATCH** ⟺ G1 passes at α/4 vs BOTH null scopes ∧ G2 ∧
  random-key row null → keys select maps across families; rung 2
  (P-STACK-1) unlocks.
- **PARTIAL-WITHIN-DOMAIN** ⟺ G1 passes within-domain but cross-domain
  keys also fire (union null fails) → map-selection exists, family
  isolation doesn't; report which keys cross-fire.
- **NOT-DISPATCHABLE** ⟺ G1 fails both scopes ∨ G2 fails → the ladder
  stops; function selection stays query-text/content-side only
  (P-ATT-MED's medium-handle remains the only steering).
- **negative/inconclusive** ⟺ gate-0 fails.

**Nulls (mandatory).** Paired shuffled-key (within + union scopes);
matched-random vector; no-key baseline; per-item real-word ceiling;
`--validate` planted worlds (indexed / flat / within-only) must
discriminate before any model run.

**Registers (λ measure).** Claim = causal dispatch (inject → which map
runs); probe = injected-key → product-register readout, causal/behavioral,
matched. Key geometry (G3) is a value-register read, advisory only.

**Honest scope.** Keys are 3-exemplar means (small-n; noise favors the
null → conservative). Neutral-prompt choice is a design degree of freedom
(recorded verbatim; margins over a closed union blunt its influence).
Injection at one token/one layer per cell — window sweep is coarse (4
depths). Hook-not-weight; two domains = the minimal cross-family pair,
not a general ISA claim. Self-decompilation (rung 0) NOT exercised here —
this run tests dispatch with hand-built keys; trace-harvested keys are the
follow-on.

**Host & order.** `--validate` → 4B smoke (reduced cells) → **verdict host
Qwen3-32B overnight in tmux main:1**. Results →
results/fn-index/qwen3-{4b,32b}/. Instrument `scripts/explore/fn_index.py`

paired_permutation + gate).

"""P-FN-INDEX — cross-family dispatch: do injected keys select WHICH map runs?

Pre-reg: mementum/knowledge/explore/program-plates-and-the-function-index.md
§P-FN-INDEX (FROZEN s292, Michael GO). The keystone of the program-plates
ladder: function choice is content-addressable iff an injected KEY selects
which resident map executes over a FIXED operand. Negative -> the ladder
stops honestly (function selection stays query-text-only).

Maps (5, two domains): geography city-of / country-of / continent-of (mh3
bank) + animals class-of / covering-of (the SECOND BANK, canonical home =
this file). Keys = mean last-token residual over 3 HELD-OUT exemplar
prompts per map minus the grand mean across maps (the "about to apply f"
state, map-level, item-independent). Dispatch cell: operand d_E installed
at its nonce slot (L_ref=9), NEUTRAL prompt naming no map, key injected at
the final token at L_inj; readout = first-token margin of f(X)'s product
over the UNION candidate set.

Conditions per cell: key_f / 4 other keys (shuffled-key null, includes
other-DOMAIN keys = the cross-family test) / matched random vector /
no-key. G1: paired permutation diagonal-vs-shuffled at 4 pre-declared
relative depths {.3,.45,.6,.75}, selection-corrected alpha/4, scored for
WITHIN-domain and UNION null scopes. G2: diagonal acc > no-key acc (the
key must FLIP the answer). Verdicts: INDEXED-DISPATCH /
PARTIAL-WITHIN-DOMAIN / NOT-DISPATCHABLE / inconclusive.

# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# Frozen verdict logic (pure; --validate exercises it)
# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
def score_layer(diag: np.ndarray, within: np.ndarray, union: np.ndarray,
                rng: np.random.Generator, alpha_sel: float) -> dict:
    """diag/within/union: per-cell margins (diag) and null-mean margins."""
    g_within = gate(float(np.mean(diag - within)),
                    paired_permutation(diag, within, rng), "greater",
                    alpha_sel, name="dispatch_within")
    g_union = gate(float(np.mean(diag - union)),
                   paired_permutation(diag, union, rng), "greater",
                   alpha_sel, name="dispatch_union")
    return {"contrast_within": g_within, "contrast_union": g_union,
            "diag_mean": float(diag.mean())}


def fn_index_verdict(gate0: bool, best: dict, g2_flip: bool,
                     rand_ok: bool) -> str:
    if not gate0:
        return "negative/inconclusive (gate-0)"
    w = best["contrast_within"].verdict
    u = best["contrast_union"].verdict
    if w and u and g2_flip and rand_ok:
        return "INDEXED-DISPATCH"
    if w and not u:
        return "PARTIAL-WITHIN-DOMAIN"
    if w and u and not (g2_flip and rand_ok):
        return "PARTIAL-WITHIN-DOMAIN"  # dispatch contrast w/o flip: partial
    return "NOT-DISPATCHABLE"


def run_validate(alpha: float) -> int:
    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
    alpha_sel = alpha / len(DEPTHS)
    print("── P-FN-INDEX --validate (planted worlds, no model) ──")
    n, noise = 90, 0.6
    ok = True

    def world(diag_mu, within_mu, union_mu, flip=True, rand_flat=True):
        diag = diag_mu + rng.normal(0, noise, n)
        within = within_mu + rng.normal(0, noise, n)
        union = union_mu + rng.normal(0, noise, n)
        best = score_layer(diag, within, union, rng, alpha_sel)
        return fn_index_verdict(True, best, flip, rand_flat)

    calls = {
        "indexed": (world(1.5, 0.0, 0.0), "INDEXED-DISPATCH"),
        "within-only": (world(1.5, 0.0, 1.4), "PARTIAL-WITHIN-DOMAIN"),
        "flat": (world(0.0, 0.0, 0.0), "NOT-DISPATCHABLE"),
        "no-flip": (world(1.5, 0.0, 0.0, flip=False), "PARTIAL-WITHIN-DOMAIN"),
    }
    for w, (call, want) in calls.items():
        good = call == want
        print(f"[G1] {w}-world -> {call} (want {want}) {'OK' if good else 'FAIL'}")
        ok &= good
    print(f"\n── --validate {'ALL PASS' if ok else 'FAIL'} ──")
    return 0 if ok else 1


# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# Model path
# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
def run_model(args) -> int:
    import operand_multihop3 as mh3
    import torch
    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer

    dev = (args.device if (args.device != "mps" or torch.backends.mps.is_available())
           else "cpu")
    rng = np.random.default_rng(args.seed)
    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(args.model_id)
    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
        args.model_id, dtype=getattr(torch, args.dtype)).to(dev).eval()
    dec, _n, _u = mh3.resolve_parts(model)
    n_layers = len(dec)
    L, S = args.ref_layer, args.scale
    inj_layers = [round(d * n_layers) for d in DEPTHS]
    alpha_sel = args.alpha / len(DEPTHS)
    print(f"[fni] {args.model_id} L_ref={L} scale={S} key_scale="
          f"{args.key_scale} dev={dev} n_layers={n_layers} "
          f"inj_layers={inj_layers}")

    nonce = NONCE_CANDS[0]
    nonce_tid = tok(" " + nonce, add_special_tokens=False).input_ids[-1]

    def first_tid(w):
        return mh3.first_tid(tok, w)

    # product vocabularies + union candidate set (collisions dropped)
    product_of = {}
    for lm in mh3.LM_LIST:
        product_of[("city", lm)] = mh3.CITY_OF[lm]
        product_of[("country", lm)] = mh3.COUNTRY_OF[lm]
        product_of[("continent", lm)] = mh3.CONT_OF[lm]
    for a in AN_LIST:
        product_of[("class", a)] = CLASS_OF[a]
        product_of[("cover", a)] = COVER_OF[a]
    vocab = (set(mh3.CITIES) | set(mh3.COUNTRIES) | set(mh3.CONTINENTS)
             | set(CLASSES) | set(COVERS))
    tid_map, drop = {}, set()
    for w in sorted(vocab):
        t = first_tid(w)
        clash = [x for x, tt in tid_map.items() if tt == t]
        if clash:
            drop.add(w)
            drop.update(clash)
        tid_map[w] = t
    union = {w: tid_map[w] for w in sorted(vocab - drop)}
    print(f"[fni] union candidates: {len(union)} (dropped collisions: "
          f"{sorted(drop)})")

    # ── ceilings ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    def real_pred(prefix, query, word, labels):
        ids = tok(prefix + query.format(x=word), return_tensors="pt").to(dev)
        with torch.no_grad():
            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()
        return max(labels, key=lambda k: lo[first_tid(k)])

    valid_geo = []
    for lm in mh3.LM_LIST:
        if (real_pred(mh3.CITY_PREFIX, mh3.CITY_QUERY, lm, mh3.CITIES)
                == mh3.CITY_OF[lm]
                and real_pred(mh3.COUNTRY_PREFIX, mh3.COUNTRY_QUERY, lm,
                              mh3.COUNTRIES) == mh3.COUNTRY_OF[lm]
                and real_pred(mh3.CONT_PREFIX, mh3.CONT_QUERY, lm,
                              mh3.CONTINENTS) == mh3.CONT_OF[lm]):
            valid_geo.append(lm)
    valid_an = []
    for a in AN_LIST:
        if (real_pred(CLASS_PREFIX, CLASS_QUERY, a, CLASSES) == CLASS_OF[a]
                and real_pred(COVER_PREFIX, COVER_QUERY, a, COVERS)
                == COVER_OF[a]):
            valid_an.append(a)
    print(f"[fni] ceilings: geo {len(valid_geo)}/18 animal {len(valid_an)}/18")
    gate0 = bool(len(valid_geo) >= 10 and len(valid_an) >= 10)
    if args.n_per_domain:
        valid_geo = valid_geo[:args.n_per_domain]

    def cell_logits(domain, item, key_vec, li):
        prompt = NEUTRAL.format(x=nonce)

        with torch.no_grad():

        for hd in handles:

    def margin_and_hit(lo, m, item):
        prod = product_of[(m, item)]
        if prod not in union:
            return None, None
        others = [t for w, t in union.items() if w != prod]
        marg = float(lo[union[prod]] - max(lo[t] for t in others))
        return marg, bool(marg > 0)

        g_rand = gate(float(np.mean(diag - np.asarray(rand_m))),

        acc_d = float(np.mean(diag_hit))

    best_li = max(per_layer,

    sc, g_rand, acc_d, acc_0 = per_layer[best_li]["_g"]

    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="P-FN-INDEX cross-family dispatch")
    ap.add_argument("--validate", action="store_true")
    ap.add_argument("--model-id", default="Qwen/Qwen3-4B")
    ap.add_argument("--device", default="mps")
    ap.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16", choices=["float32", "bfloat16"])
    ap.add_argument("--ref-layer", type=int, default=9)
    ap.add_argument("--scale", type=float, default=2.0)
    ap.add_argument("--key-scale", type=float, default=2.0)
    ap.add_argument("--n-per-domain", type=int, default=0,
                    help="cap items per domain (0 = all valid; smoke uses 6)")
    ap.add_argument("--alpha", type=float, default=0.05)
    ap.add_argument("--seed", type=int, default=0)
    ap.add_argument("--out", default="results/fn-index/qwen3-4b")
    args = ap.parse_args()
    if args.validate:
        return run_validate(args.alpha)
    return run_model(args)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    raise SystemExit(main())

### Thinking

I'm seeing the core mechanics of the fn_index instrument now—how the operand direction gets installed at a specific nonce slot through a hook, how the map key is injected at the final token position, and the neutral prompt structure that frames the task.

The new challenge is composing two indexed exposures: I need to inject two keys at different depths to verify that h(g(X)) produces the expected output, where g(X) becomes the operand feeding into h. The issue is that in the fn_index setup, a map's product is a word (like "country" from country-of applied to a landmark), but for composition to work, that output needs to be a valid operand for the next map. Geography maps like city-of and country-of don't naturally compose since they expect landmarks as input, not countries—though the mh3 bank might offer different composable structures. The stack is depth-ordered, so g's key gets injected early and h's key later, letting g compute first and then h operates on that result. I'm setting up two control conditions: one swaps the depth order to see if the composition breaks, and another mismatches the types between the maps—like having g produce a country but h expect a landmark input—which should fail the composability check.

Now I need to verify mechanically how two keys actually compose. When g's key is installed, it sets up a map at a fixed direction slot, and when h's key is installed afterward, h should read g's output as its operand. The empirical test will confirm whether injecting keys at ordered windows actually produces this composition chain. I'm grounding this in the mh3 bank with a concrete example using landmarks.

For a well-typed composition, I have landmark → country → continent: starting with "Eiffel Tower," country-of maps it to France, then continent-of maps France to Europe. This is the canonical multi-hop that mh3 was designed to handle. But if I try a type-mismatched composition like country → city, where city-of expects a landmark but receives a country instead, the system should fail gracefully since a country isn't a valid landmark operand.

I need to check the actual operand_multihop3 structure to see what compositional hops are available and what ground truth data exists. This is critical for setting up the pre-registration gates correctly.

### Thinking

The mh3 bank organizes landmarks with their associated city, country, and continent, then maps relationships between these levels—connecting landmarks to countries and cities, countries to continents, and cities to continents through a series of hop functions.

For the 3-hop composition, I'm chaining these maps together: landmark flows through to city, then through additional transformations to reach the continent. The 2-hop compositions work differently—one path takes a landmark directly to its country, while another connects a city to its continent. The key insight is that the composed maps in fn_index are all landmark-operand functions, but for the P-STACK-1 composition test, I need intermediate maps that operate on countries (not landmarks), so the country-to-continent map becomes the critical h function that takes the output of g as its input.

The swap_bridge machinery from the 3-hop capacity work already handles bridge-swapping mechanically, but P-STACK-1 shifts the approach: instead of swapping the bridge value itself, I inject keys that select which map executes at each window position, effectively composing them. This aligns with the pre-registration sketch—I'm placing two indexed exposures in their windows within a single context, verifying the composed product, with controls testing wrong-window placements and type mismatches that should fail gracefully through the JOIN-TYPED mechanism.

For the concrete design: I'll install a fixed landmark operand at a nonce slot (L_ref=9), use a neutral prompt, then inject two keys at different depths—key(g) at window w_g and key(h) at window w_h where w_g < w_h. The first key selects the country-of map from fn_index, and the second selects the continent-of map. The critical test is whether the model composes these: does it read continent as continent-of(country-of(landmark))? Since the landmark's continent is defined as its country's continent by construction, the composed readout should match.

But here's the issue: if h is the direct continent-of-landmark map, then injecting just key(h) alone already gives the right answer without needing g's output—so composition isn't actually being tested. To genuinely test composition, h must be a country-to-continent map instead, so that h requires g's output (the country) as input. Then key(g) alone would be ill-typed over the landmark, and only the two-key sequence would produce the correct continent.

I need to build a new country-to-continent key using held-out exemplars, then set up controls: testing h alone over the landmark (which should fail or be weak), and testing the full composition chain. type-mismatched should fail more severely than well-typed cases, though the failure is graded rather than a hard block. I'm also testing null keys and random vectors at both injection windows, then measuring the first-token margin of the composed product (continent) against a candidate set that includes continents, countries, cities, and other entities to see if the model picks the right level or stops at an intermediate step like country. I'm setting up gates that mirror the function index structure: Gate-0 checks that the landmark-to-country and country-to-continent links hold with valid ground truth and sane key norms, while G1 (the primary composition gate) compares the composed margin when both keys are present and correctly ordered against other configurations.

G1 evaluates whether composition actually beats the strongest single-key control by testing paired permutations across a grid of window positions, using selection-corrected significance. G2 checks that composed accuracy on the continent target exceeds both the best single-key accuracy and the no-key baseline, ensuring the two-key stack produces answers the individual maps don't. G3 (secondary, type-discipline gate) verifies that well-typed composition margins outperform type-mismatched ones in paired comparisons.

The mismatch failure itself is graded rather than binary—type-mismatched composition still beats random, and I'm reporting that gradient. The window-pair profile maps the schedule of (w_g, w_h) positions, and wrong-window selections should fail cleanly. STACKABLE is locked when G1 and G2 both pass, wrong-window fails, and random null stays clean, unlocking rung 3 (P-BAKE-STACK) to build programs from indexed parts. TYPED-STACKABLE adds the G3 constraint—the type-mismatch gradient must fail gradedly—confirming the linker's composability constraint is real.

ORDER-FREE or BAG composition happens when the composed result beats individual parts but wrong-window also works, meaning composition isn't depth-ordered by the PC schedule—a weaker positive signal. NOT-STACKABLE triggers when G1 fails or the composed result matches the best single part, meaning two indexed exposures don't compose in-context and the ladder stops at single dispatch, so programs need weight-baking rather than in-context stacking. Negative or inconclusive results come from gate-0 failure. I'm now looking at the honest scope and registers, specifically the causal composition register where I inject two parts and probe the composed product.

The key injection produces a readable product register with causal and behavioral matching. I need to validate against nulls—g-alone, h-alone, wrong-window ordering, type mismatches, random noise, and no-key cases—to discriminate between composition, single-only, order-free, and mismatch-graded outcomes before running the model. Two-key injection is more perturbative than one due to interaction confounding, so I'm controlling via single-key arms and random-both conditions. The windows are coarse, and the h=country→continent key is a new build with held-out exemplars and ceiling-gating. I'm testing in-context composition only, not weights, on one well-typed chain, so generality is a follow-up question.

Rather than forking, I should extend the existing fn_index machinery by creating a new instrument script that imports the key exemplars and construction logic from fn_index, plus the country-to-continent mapping and hook-injection utilities from mh3—reusing the same pattern fn_index uses with its dependencies. This keeps the architecture consistent while handling the country→continent key, two-window injection, and composed readout that P-STACK-1 needs.

But the immediate task is to draft the pre-reg section for Michael's freeze approval, not build the instrument yet. The workflow is: draft pre-reg → Michael freezes → then build and validate. So I need to write §P-STACK-1 and present it for his sign-off. I'll present the full draft in-chat for Michael's approval, and once he gives the freeze signal, I'll write it to the page and commit. I need to flag the key design decisions clearly since those are his calls, but he asked me to draft it, so I'll make my best judgment calls and let him adjust. Let me also verify that COUNTRY_CONT and CITY_CONT are properly referenced in the codebase before finalizing.

I'm thinking through the cleanest way to compose the geographic mappings: using landmark→country via COUNTRY_OF, then country→continent via COUNTRY_CONT, which gives me the composed landmark→continent relationship. The type safety matters here—if I try to apply country2cont directly to a landmark, it should fail since landmarks aren't countries, so I need to ensure the composition is explicit and well-typed.

Now I'm considering how to test the type-checking gradient. If I use city-of instead (landmark→city), then applying country2cont to a city name is a near-miss—both cities and countries are geographic places, so it's a graded type mismatch rather than catastrophic. But if I use something like animal class-of (landmark→mammal), then applying country2cont to an animal class is a far-mismatch that should fail completely. This gives me a nice spectrum: well-typed (country) → near-mismatch (city) → far-mismatch (animal class) → random, which is exactly what I need to test the graded prediction behavior.

For the window depth testing, I'm setting up a grid of ordered pairs from the available depths {0.3, 0.45, 0.6, 0.75}, keeping it to about 3-4 pairs like (0.3, 0.6), (0.3, 0.75), (0.45, 0.75) to avoid selection correction overhead. The operand gets installed early at a fixed reference level, then the two windows are positioned later in the stack.

The fn_index dispatch shows a U-shaped pattern working best at the early and late positions, so I'm constraining w_g to the early depths {0.3, 0.45} and w_h to the late depths {0.6, 0.75}, giving me 4 ordered pairs with selection-corrected significance levels.

Now I'm structuring the pre-registration to match the format of the previous section for consistency, covering all the standard components like claims, maps, keys, conditions, gates, and verdict. I'm also deciding on the readout union to include continents, countries, and cities so I can track where the process stops.

The core empirical question is whether injecting a key at a late window allows h to execute over g's output, which the stack-depth theory predicts should happen but the honest null says shouldn't be stackable—a clean falsifiable prediction.

The subtlety here is that h needs g's computed country value as its operand, but in this single-token nonce setup, g's output isn't installed as a fresh operand slot; instead, h must operate on whatever the residual stream now carries after g has written the country value into it. This works if g writes before h's window, and the theory suggests that for a two-operation sequence in one forward pass, inline execution is possible when the depth fits within available capacity.

We've already proven that three-hop composition works natively in the model, so the real question becomes whether two injected keys can actually program that composition over a neutral prompt—where the model isn't explicitly instructed to perform the two-hop. This is the clean test: native three-hop works, single dispatch works, so can we assemble the two-hop from two injected keys alone, where neither key in isolation would be sufficient?

## §P-STACK-1 — the seam test: do two indexed keys compose in-context? (PRE-REG DRAFT — freeze on Michael GO)

**Claim under test.** A program is a depth-ordered stack of indexed exposures (`program ≡ depth_ordered_stack | PC ≡ window`). Test the minimal case: over a FIXED operand X, injecting key(g) at an early window and key(h) at a later window yields the **composed** product h(g(X)) — where h alone is ill-typed on X, so composition is doing real work. Negative → two indexed exposures don't stack in-context; program-plates need weight-baking (P-BAKE-STACK), not in-context assembly. The ladder pauses at rung 1 (single dispatch).

**The chain (well-typed, one substrate, ground-truthed by mh3).**
- **g = country-of** (landmark → country); key = fn_index `country` (reused 1:1).
- **h = country→continent** (`COUNTRY_CONT`); key = **NEW** held-out `country2cont` exemplar-mean (built like all fn_index keys: 3 held-out country exemplars, "The {x} is on the continent of", minus grand mean; ceiling-gated).
- **composed truth** = `CONT_OF[X]` (the landmark's continent = its country's continent, by construction).
- **why it discriminates:** key(h) alone over a landmark is **ill-typed** (h expects a country) → h-alone should fail; key(g) alone yields the *country* (wrong readout register). Only the ordered stack lands the continent.

**Cell.** Operand X = landmark installed as d_lm·S at its nonce slot (L_ref=9, frozen mh3 machinery); NEUTRAL prompt ("Consider the {nonce}. The answer is" — names no map, no task); key(g) injected at final token at window w_g, key(h) at w_h, with **w_g < w_h** (depth-ordered). Readout = first-token margin of `CONT_OF[X]` over the UNION set (continents ∪ countries ∪ cities ∪ animal-products, collisions dropped) — the union lets us detect "stopped at g" (lands on country) vs "composed" (lands on continent). Cells = valid landmarks (mh3 `links_ok`).

**Conditions per cell.**
| arm | what | prediction if STACKABLE |
|---|---|---|
| **stack** (g@w_g, h@w_h) | the program | lands continent ✅ |
| **g-alone** | key(g) only | lands country (not continent) |
| **h-alone** | key(h) only | ill-typed, fails |
| **wrong-window** (h@w_g, g@w_h) | order reversed | fails (PC is real) |
| **type-mismatch-near** (g′=city-of, h) | city→country2cont | GRADED fail (near type) |
| **type-mismatch-far** (g′=animal class-of, h) | mammal→country2cont | catastrophic fail |
| **random-both** | matched-norm noise ×2 | null |
| **no-key** | operand only | baseline |

**Windows.** Pre-declared ordered grid respecting the measured U-shape (early composition band, late readout): w_g ∈ {0.3, 0.45}, w_h ∈ {0.6, 0.75} → **4 ordered pairs**; verdict pair = best composed contrast, **selection-corrected α/4 = 0.0125**.

**Gates (frozen).**
- **Gate-0:** mh3 links valid (landmark→country, country→continent, composed landmark→continent ceilings pass) ∧ `country2cont` key norm sane.
- **G1 (primary, COMPOSITION):** stack margin vs **best single-key part** (paired permutation over cells, per window-pair): `stack − max(g-alone, h-alone)`. Composition beats its parts ⟺ the stack computes h∘g, not merely the better map. Passes at α/4.
- **G2 (flip):** stack accuracy (lands continent) > best single-key accuracy **and** > no-key. The two-key program produces what neither part does.
- **G3 (type-discipline, secondary):** stack(well-typed) > type-mismatch (paired), **and** failure is GRADED: well-typed > near-mismatch > far-mismatch > random (monotone ladder = the linker's `composable(g,h) ⟺ product(g) ∈ key_passband(h)` made behavioral, per JOIN-TYPED). Report the gradient; a hard cliff instead of a ramp is itself informative.
- **Advisory (never gated):** window-pair profile (the PC schedule); order-sensitivity magnitude (wrong-window drop); fraction "stopped at g."

**Verdict (frozen).**
- **TYPED-STACKABLE** ⟺ G1 ∧ G2 ∧ wrong-window fails ∧ random clean ∧ **G3 graded** → indexed parts compose in depth order under type discipline; **rung 3 (P-BAKE-STACK) unlocks.**
- **STACKABLE (untyped)** ⟺ G1 ∧ G2 ∧ wrong-window fails, but G3 flat (mismatch composes as well as match) → composition is real but type-blind at the seam; report (tension with JOIN-TYPED — richest branch).
- **ORDER-FREE / BAG** ⟺ stack > parts but wrong-window **also** works → composition without a program counter (no depth ordering) → weaker positive.
- **NOT-STACKABLE** ⟺ G1 fails ∨ stack ≈ best single part → no in-context assembly; program-plates require weight-baking. Ladder pauses honestly.
- **negative/inconclusive** ⟺ gate-0 fails.

**Nulls (mandatory).** Single-key parts (g-alone, h-alone); wrong-window (order); type-mismatch near+far; random-both; no-key; per-landmark mh3 ceilings; `--validate` planted worlds (composes / single-only / order-free / mismatch-graded) must discriminate before any model run.

**Registers (λ measure).** Claim = causal composition (two injections → composed product); probe = two-key injection → product-register readout, causal/behavioral, matched. Key geometry advisory only.

**Honest scope (pre-committed).** Two-key injection is more perturbative than one (interaction confound → controlled by single-key arms + random-both). Windows coarse (4 pairs). `country2cont` key is a NEW build (ceiling-gated, held-out). Hook-not-weight — in-context assembly only; weight-baking is P-BAKE-STACK. ONE well-typed chain (geo country→continent); generality (animal chains, 3-stacks) is the follow-on. Native 3-hop is known to work (mh3) — this tests whether *injected keys* drive it over a neutral prompt, not whether the model *can* compose.

**Host & order.** `--validate` → 4B smoke (reduced cells) → verdict host **Qwen3-32B** overnight tmux main:1. Instrument = **new `scripts/explore/fn_stack.py`**, importing (no fork) `fn_index` (KEY_EXEMPLARS, product/union build), `operand_multihop3` (COUNTRY_CONT, CONT_OF, build_dirs, add_hook_at, links_ok), `verbum.dsp` (gate, paired_permutation). Results → `results/fn-stack/qwen3-{4b,32b}/`.

---

**Design calls flagged for your freeze (fn_index-style):**
1. **G1 baseline = "beats best single part"** (not "beats no-key"). Stronger — the stack must beat its own components, killing the "h-alone already works" confound. Your call if you'd rather also require beating no-key (I have it in G2).
2. **Type gradient uses cross-domain far-mismatch** (animal class → country2cont) for the catastrophic end. Alternative: keep it within-geo (city vs country only) for a purer graded ramp.
3. **4 ordered window-pairs** (α/4). Could go to 3 (drop 0.45→0.6) to buy sensitivity, or expand if you want a finer PC map.

Want me to **freeze this** (write §P-STACK-1 to the program-plates page + commit) and proceed to build `fn_stack.py` + `--validate`? Or adjust any of the three design calls first?

def first_tid(tok, w):
    return tok(" " + w, add_special_tokens=False).input_ids[0]


def cap_hook(store, li):
    def hook(_m, _i, out):
        h = out[0] if isinstance(out, tuple) else out
        store[li] = h.detach().float().cpu().numpy()
    return hook


def add_hook_at(vec_t, pos):
    def hook(_m, _i, out):
        h = out[0] if isinstance(out, tuple) else out
        if 0 <= pos < h.shape[1]:
            h[0, pos, :] = h[0, pos, :] + vec_t.to(h.dtype)
        return out
    return hook


def resolve_parts(model):
    """(decoder-layers, final-norm, lm_head) across architectures (dense + hybrid)."""
    inner = model.model
    lm = inner if hasattr(inner, "layers") else inner.language_model
    return lm.layers, lm.norm, model.lm_head


def main() -> None:
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("--model-id", default="Qwen/Qwen3-4B")
    ap.add_argument("--ref-layer", type=int, default=9)          # install layer
    ap.add_argument("--scale", type=float, default=2.0)
    ap.add_argument("--swap-layers", type=int, nargs="+", default=[11, 15, 20])
    ap.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16", choices=["float32", "bfloat16"])
    ap.add_argument("--device", default="mps")
    ap.add_argument("--mode", default="full", choices=["ceiling", "full"])
    ap.add_argument("--out", default="results/ffn-bake/operand-multihop3-qwen3-4b")
    args = ap.parse_args()

    L = args.ref_layer
    dev = (args.device if (args.device != "mps" or torch.backends.mps.is_available())
           else "cpu")
    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(args.model_id)
    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
        args.model_id, dtype=getattr(torch, args.dtype)).to(dev).eval()
    dec, norm_f, unembed = resolve_parts(model)
    S = args.scale

    cont_ids = {c: first_tid(tok, c) for c in CONTINENTS}
    country_ids = {c: first_tid(tok, c) for c in COUNTRIES}
    city_ids = {c: first_tid(tok, c) for c in CITIES}
    nonce_last = tok(" " + NONCE, add_special_tokens=False).input_ids[-1]
    print(f"[mh3] {args.model_id} L={L} scale={S} dev={dev} "
          f"n={NONCE!r} mode={args.mode}")

    def find_slot(ids_list):
        idx = [i for i, t in enumerate(ids_list) if t == nonce_last]
        return idx[-1] if idx else len(ids_list) - 1

    def pred_over(prefix, query, word, label_ids, adds=None):
        """argmax over a CLOSED candidate set (first-token logits) at nonce slot."""
        prompt = prefix + query.format(x=word)
        ids = tok(prompt, return_tensors="pt").to(dev)
        slot = find_slot(ids.input_ids[0].tolist())
        handles = []
        for (li, vec) in (adds or []):
            vt = torch.tensor(vec, dtype=torch.float32, device=dev)
            handles.append(dec[li].register_forward_hook(add_hook_at(vt, slot)))
        with torch.no_grad():
            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()
        for hd in handles:
            hd.remove()
        return max(label_ids, key=lambda k: lo[label_ids[k]])

    def cont_pred(word, adds=None):
        return pred_over(CONT_PREFIX, CONT_QUERY, word, cont_ids, adds)

    def country_pred(word, adds=None):
        return pred_over(COUNTRY_PREFIX, COUNTRY_QUERY, word, country_ids, adds)

    def city_pred(word, adds=None):
        return pred_over(CITY_PREFIX, CITY_QUERY, word, city_ids, adds)

    # ── S1 links (real word, no install) = the knowledge ceiling ──────────────────
    link_lm_city = {lm: int(city_pred(lm) == CITY_OF[lm]) for lm in LM_LIST}
    link_city_country = {
