### Thinking

I've got the building blocks in place: the country-to-continent prefix and query already exist in mh3, which I can use both for constructing the key and handling ceilings. I have the country-to-continent mapping dictionary ready, and I need to decide whether to use the build_dirs function from mh3 or replicate the approach from fn_index for building operand directions.

For the landmark directions, I'll follow fn_index's pattern of pooling over frames and capturing at the last token position. The key construction will mirror fn_index's approach too—I'll use exemplar prompts with held-out countries formatted through the country-to-continent query, making sure those exemplar countries (like Portugal, Japan, Kenya) don't overlap with the test landmark countries (Spain, China, Tanzania, Saudi Arabia, Malaysia, etc.). I need to verify that Japan isn't already in the test set before finalizing the exemplars. Now I'm seeing the distinction between how operands and keys are positioned in the model. The operand gets placed at the nonce slot, but the keys are injected at the final token position—these aren't always the same location. For the composition setup with two functions, both g and h have their keys at the final token, but I need to track where the operand actually sits in the architecture.

For the readout mechanism, I'm looking at how the first-token margin distinguishes the correct country from a union of all possible classes (continents, countries, cities, animal products). This setup lets the model stop at g's output when needed. Now I'm designing the gate and verdict logic to align with the pre-registration, setting up the validation framework with specific contrast tests across window pairs and margin comparisons.

Writing verdict logic... Defining the verdict conditions... So I'm settling on g3_typed as the core type-discipline signal (stack beats mismatch significantly) and g3_graded as the monotone ordering pattern (well > near > far > random). Both are needed for TYPED-STACKABLE to hold. Now I'm setting up test worlds for validation with specific parameter configurations—composing scenarios where stacking performs well with low noise and mismatch penalties, contrasted against single-cell baselines to confirm the stacking advantage.

I also need to thread accuracy metrics (stack_acc, single_acc, nokey_acc) as explicit scalars through the planted worlds rather than computing them implicitly.

Now I'm structuring the verdict logic so that scoring over a window pair returns a dict containing the contrast gates, with a top-level verdict function that takes the necessary booleans as inputs. I'll also handle the G2 flip parameter in planted worlds by passing flip=True/False.

Writing fn_stack.py... Writing the key construction... I'll capture each key at all four depth-layers to ensure they're available regardless of which window they're injected into. For each cell forward pass, I'm setting up four scenarios: the stack case adds both country and country2cont keys at their respective layers, while the g-alone and h-alone cases add only one key each, and the wrong-window case swaps which key goes to which layer.

I'm also testing mismatch cases where I inject mismatched keys (city or class instead of the correct ones), a random-both case with noise, and a no-key baseline. Then I'll compute the readout margin of CONT_OF over the union and use argmax to detect whether the model stopped at the g-head. I'm writing a helper function that takes a list of (layer, vector) pairs to add, always installing the operand at the nonce slot and then adding keys at the final token position.

Writing the hook setup... No collision risk since the hook pairs always use distinct layers, and the reference layer stays separate. The operand hook fires first during the forward pass, then the key hooks at later layers, preserving the causal order. Now I'm building the readout union by combining vocabulary from continents, countries, cities, classes, and covers, filtering out first-token collisions, then computing the margin between the product's score and the maximum of all others.

For the landmark's composed answer, I'm checking that the chain holds: the country prediction matches what we expect from the landmark, and the country-to-continent prediction matches the lookup table, using the existing model's real-word predictions to verify both the intermediate and final steps.

I'm setting up validation so only landmarks that pass all three checks (landmark→country, country→continent, and the composed landmark→continent) count toward the gate threshold of at least 10 valid landmarks.

Now I'm implementing the scoring function that takes a stack of results and compares them against the best single result using a permutation test to determine which window pair produces the strongest contrast. Now I'm defining a verdict function that evaluates whether the stack is truly stackable based on a series of gates—checking if it passes the initial gate, if it's stackable at all, if order matters, if it beats random performance, and whether it exhibits type discipline, ultimately classifying it as typed-stackable, untyped-stackable, order-free, or not stackable. The untyped case stacks well with a score of 1.5, but the mismatch penalty (mnear) equals the stack score, so it's not monotone graded—that makes it untyped stackable rather than typed. Now I need to update the helper function to work with arrays derived from the mean values.

I'm setting up the dtype handling for the hooks—the float32 tensors get cast to match the hidden state dtype when added, which mh3 handles automatically. For the nonce constant, I can either import NONCE_CANDS from holo_cap or just set mh3.NONCE directly. So those imports should be safe without torch at the module level. Now I'm writing fn_stack.py, and I'll need to replicate fn_index's build_dirs function (which pools over frames and captures landmarks at position L) since it's defined as a closure inside run_model and isn't importable. I'll mirror that logic exactly to keep the operand construction consistent.

last)))\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            lo = model(**ids).logits[0, -1, :].float().cpu().numpy()\n        for hd in handles:\n            hd.remove()\n        return lo\n\n    def for t in others))\n        argw = max(union, key=lambda w: lo[union[w]])\n        return marg, bool(marg > 0), argw\n\n    cells = [lm for lm in valid if mh3.CONT_OF[lm] in union]\n    print(f\"[stk] (lh, kh)])\n            lo_r = cell_logits(lm, [(lg, rand_like(kg)), (lh, rand_like(kh))])\n            lo_0 = cell_logits(lm, [])\n            ms, hs, aw = margin_hit(lo_s, prod)\n            mg, _, ### Thinking

The project uses per-file ignores to suppress RUF001/002/003 checks for files using the domain alphabet. fn_index.py passes all checks despite not being in the ignore list, which means its docstring must avoid the ambiguous characters like ∪ and ×. My fn_stack docstring uses those characters, so it's triggering RUF002.

I have two options: add fn_stack to the per-file ignores or rewrite the docstring to use ASCII equivalents. Following the principle that fixing is better than suppressing, I'll rewrite the docstring to replace ∪ with "union of" and × with "x" — these symbols are just decorative in prose, not essential domain notation. I'll also remove the unused CLASS_OF module-level variable to keep things clean.

Rewriting the docstring... I need to search through the entire file to find all instances of the union and multiplication symbols that need replacing, since the grep output was truncated and might have missed some occurrences outside the initial lines.

I'm dealing with an import ordering issue where ruff's isort rules want me to reorganize how I'm importing from `fn_index` and `verbum.dsp`. The results are striking—the h-alone model with the country-to-continent key actually achieves 0.88 accuracy on the raw landmark data, which contradicts the expectation that it should fail. The control conditions all fail as expected, and interestingly, even reversing the window order doesn't significantly degrade performance. What's happening is that the 4B model has internalized a direct landmark-to-continent mapping—the country2cont key, despite its name, is really functioning as a "output continent" signal that triggers the model to produce the continent of the landmark directly, rather than routing through an intermediate country representation. This reveals a design flaw: the h-key is essentially hijacking the continent-output pathway rather than performing the type-mismatched operation we assumed it would.

The real issue is that both the composed path (country→continent) and the direct shortcut (landmark→continent) produce the same continent token, making them indistinguishable in the output. My original hypothesis that h-alone would fail on a landmark operand because it's ill-typed turns out to be false—there's a single-hop resident map that bypasses the type mismatch entirely. The key doesn't actually enforce that the operand is treated as a country; it just signals "output a continent," which the model can satisfy directly from the landmark. So the smoke test reveals the confound rather than cleanly demonstrating non-compositionality.

To fix this, I'd need to either change the target so the composed answer isn't directly reachable from the operand, use a composition whose result doesn't exist as a resident map, or break the h-alone shortcut by making h strictly require country-level input—though that last option is hard because the key's "output continent" signal still lets the landmark→continent path fire. The discriminator test shows h-alone does land the right continent with 0.88 accuracy, confirming the leak.

The real issue is that the operand (landmark) has a direct edge to the target (continent), so I need to design g and h such that h(g(X)) doesn't match any single resident map. The deeper problem is that this geography knowledge base is transitively closed — every 2-hop composition's endpoint is also reachable in 1 hop, which means h-alone can always short-circuit the composition. This makes it a poor substrate for testing whether composition actually does work that the individual parts can't, since the parts alone are already sufficient. I need a knowledge base where g(X) produces something that isn't directly reachable.

A better approach: have g map from landmark to country, then h map from country to something the landmark doesn't directly have — like the country's capital, currency, or language. ### Thinking

The 32B model actually resolved to TYPED-STACKABLE, which is different from what the 4B produced—that earlier result was just a compression artifact. Looking at the scoring, the best pair comes from positions 29-38, but I need to make sure I'm evaluating this correctly by checking which pairing has the highest compose_vs_parts value. The stack's relative improvement over individual parts is statistically significant, but that's misleading—the absolute accuracy is only 6%, meaning the model almost never produces the correct continent. The gates pass because they measure relative margin gains (stack at −2.688 vs parts at −6.705 and −5.110), not actual compositional success. The flip gate trivially passes since 0.06 > 0.00, but that's just a single cell landing correctly. So TYPED-STACKABLE is technically valid by the frozen criteria, yet it masks that nothing is actually working in absolute terms.

Looking at the late h-window (L48), the shortcut partially recovers—h-alone reaches 28% accuracy and stack manages 33–39%, so the stack barely edges it out. But at the early h-window (L38), h-alone completely fails (0% accuracy, mean −5.1), and stack only reaches 6% despite the statistical significance. The honest picture is that the verdict holds on relative margins, but the effect is confined to negative territory where composition barely functions. than either alone, while the type-mismatch controls show progressively weaker margins, creating a clean monotonic gradient from the graded stack down through near and far mismatches to random. The composition mechanism is real and disciplined by type, but it's too weak in absolute terms to actually flip the model's prediction toward the continent except in rare cases.

The key insight is that the window matters: when I inject the h-key at the readout layer (L48), it produces a stronger signal because that's where continent information naturally emerges, giving h-alone an accuracy of 0.28 through a shortcut pathway. But at the composition layer (L38), h is weaker in isolation even though the composition-relative signal remains strong.

At L48, the stack barely outperforms h-alone (0.33-0.39 versus 0.28), suggesting a shortcut regime similar to what I saw in 4B. At L38, h-alone completely fails (0.00 accuracy) while the stack shows the strongest relative advantage with statistical significance. The frozen verdict selects the typed-stackable pair based on maximum g1 score, but I need to be honest about what this means: the improvement is relative, not absolute — we're working with sub-floor margins around 6% composition accuracy and a margin so thin it hinges on a single-cell flip. The type-graded ordering is clean and monotonic, and the typed version passes, but the order-sensitivity remains a concern.

The order matters significantly (ranging from +1.788 to +3.932 in the metric), though the wrong-window case collapses entirely. This is a critical moment where I need to distinguish between relative significance and absolute compositionality — the gate is technically satisfied, but "looks significant relative" doesn't mean "actually composes." The frozen verdict says typed-stackable, but the honest characterization is that this is weak, marginal typed composition. The two-key stack does push toward h(g(X)) more than either component alone and respects the type ladder, yet it rarely wins the argmax across the accuracy window (0.06-0.39 depending on conditions). Now I'm reconsidering whether the frozen verdict should even count given the pre-registration's G2 flip. The caveat is that this effect lives in the negative margin zone—the absolute accuracy at the winning pair is only around 6%, a single-cell flip. The stacked approach does push the model toward the composed answer more reliably than either key alone (the p-value holds at 0.0001), and the type-graded structure is clean and monotonic, with order sensitivity confirming the mechanism. But the model rarely actually wins the argmax—it's moving in the right direction without crossing the finish line. At 32B, the composition window shows the h-alone signal completely drops out, which contradicts what we saw at 4B. The composition signal is real but marginal—only 6% accuracy—which means the mechanism works in principle but isn't strong enough to reliably bake into the stack. The honest assessment is that while I've demonstrated the typed, ordered composition exists, I need to strengthen it by removing the readout-window shortcut that's currently competing with the composed answer, likely through a cleaner task like country-to-capital that would let the composition actually win.

Looking at the distribution, the margins being negative across the board suggests the argmax is landing on something other than the target continent most of the time—roughly 22% on the intermediate country, 6% on the continent, and the remaining 72% scattered across other union words. That 72% landing elsewhere is the real problem, not just the country stealing the signal.

Now I need to draft the results section for the 32B model, prepare a memory candidate, get approval from Michael with an honest assessment of the composition weakness, then commit the data and update the state file.

### Thinking

I'm preparing to present the results with an important caveat and draft the analysis for Michael's approval. The key finding is that the typed-stackable configuration performs best at the L29→L38 pair, but there's a critical issue: at 32B scale, the single-key approach completely fails (accuracy drops to 0.00 with a mean of −5.11), whereas the typed version handles it correctly—the model refuses the malformed input as expected.

Looking at the window structure, I see two distinct regimes. At the composition window (h@L38), the single key is dead, g1 shows strong significance (p=0.0001), and types matter with order sensitivity, though stacking only achieves 6% accuracy. At the readout window (h@L48), the single key partially recovers (28% accuracy via shortcuts), but g1 becomes non-significant. The type hierarchy is clean and monotonic—well-typed performs best, then near-typed, far-typed, and random in descending order. Order sensitivity is substantial, adding 1.79 to 3.93 points depending on configuration, while wrong-window placement kills performance entirely. About 22% of cases land on the intermediate representation at the verdict pair.

The honest assessment is that this is a marginal, weak typed-stackable effect—all the mechanistic signatures are present and show robust aggregate margins, but the two-key stack rarely wins the argmax in absolute terms, with composition accuracy stuck near the floor at 6%. The mechanism exists but remains weak in context, which strengthens the case for weight-baking as an alternative approach.

**Frozen verdict: TYPED-STACKABLE** (best pair L29→L38: G1 p=0.0001, G2 flip, order-sensitive, graded types, vs-random clean). **The 4B→32B flip is real and in the predicted direction (reading B).**

**The 4B shortcut died at the composition window.** At 4B, h-alone (country2cont key over a landmark) landed the continent at acc 0.88 via the direct shortcut. At 32B, at the **composition window (h@L38)**, h-alone is **dead — acc 0.00, mean −5.11**. The typed 32B refuses the ill-typed single key; only the *stack* carries the composed signal. This is the ~5th 4B→32B flip of the arc (4B inlines/shortcuts; 32B is typed and literal).

**Two window regimes — the window IS the finding:**
| pair | h-alone acc | stack acc | g1 (stack−best) | reading |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| L29→**L38** (composition) | **0.00** | 0.06 | **+2.28, p=1e-4** | typed composition, shortcut dead |
| L29→**L48** (readout) | 0.28 | 0.33 | +0.10, n.s. | shortcut co-fires, stack ≈ parts |

Coheres with the U-shaped FN-INDEX window and FRAG's early-band/readout-late split: composition lives early/mid, the direct shortcut lives at readout.

**Mechanism signatures — all present and robust:**
- **Composition:** stack margin > best single part, p=0.0001 (relative, aggregate).
- **Type discipline (clean monotone ladder):** well −2.69 > near −5.22 > far −6.18 > random −6.71. The JOIN-TYPED linker prediction holds — mismatched keys compose *gradedly worse*.
- **Order:** wrong-window dead (−4.48); order_matters +1.79→+3.93. The program counter is real.

**⚠️ λ yardstick caveat (the verbatim finding that outran the gate — must lead the synthesis):** TYPED-STACKABLE passed on **relative margin improvements over sub-floor NEGATIVE margins**. Absolute composition accuracy is **at floor — ~6% (1/18) at the verdict pair** (razor-thin flip), rising to 33–39% only at the readout window where the shortcut co-fires. At the verdict pair, 22% of cells "stop at g" (land on the intermediate country), 6% reach the continent, ~72% land elsewhere. So: **the two-key stack demonstrably pushes toward h(g(X)) more than either key alone, gradedly by type, sensitive to order — but it rarely *wins* the argmax.** The seam *exists* and is *typed*, but is **weak in-context.**

**What this means for the ladder:** TYPED-STACKABLE nominally unlocks rung 3 (P-BAKE-STACK), but the weakness (6% absolute) says the in-context stack isn't yet a *reliable* program — which actually **strengthens the case that programs must be weight-baked** (P-BAKE-STACK) to be robust, and motivates the **shortcut-free chain** (country→capital) to measure composition without the readout-window shortcut competing.

### Thinking

I'm sorting through the landmarks by whether they're in their country's capital city—those that are get excluded as shortcuts, while the others remain candidates. So I've identified 10 landmarks where the famous site is in a different city than the capital, which gives me a solid sample size for testing. The effect I saw earlier was quite strong, so even with 10 cases this should be enough to detect the pattern.

Now I'm mapping out the ground truth: these landmarks span 8 countries total, with Egypt appearing three times. I need to compile the full list of capitals—Madrid, New Delhi, Riyadh, Phnom Penh, Abu Dhabi, Cairo, Rabat, Lusaka—and also include the direct cities where the landmarks actually are to see if the model shortcuts to those instead.

For multi-token capitals like New Delhi, Phnom Penh, and Abu Dhabi, I'm tracking the first token separately since that's what gets graded, and those first tokens (New, Phnom, Abu) are distinct enough to avoid collisions. I'm also setting up held-out exemplars from countries not in the test set—like Portugal→Lisbon, Japan→Tokyo, Kenya→Nairobi—to use in the prompt template variations like "The capital of {country} is" or "The country of {x} has capital." I'll stick with "The capital of {x} is" as the template since it cleanly captures the residual right before emitting the answer. Setting up the exemplars with Portugal, Japan, and Kenya as held-out examples, then treating this as a refinement to the original framework—a sibling verdict addressing the shortcut confound rather than a completely separate case.

For this new variant, the chain becomes landmark→country→capital with the composed truth being capital-of(country-of(landmark)). I'm restricting to landmarks that aren't capitals themselves to eliminate the shortcut, and expanding the readout to include capitals, cities, countries, and continents so I can distinguish between reaching the composed answer versus stopping at an intermediate step or taking the direct shortcut. The country→capital key is the new h-key, and the critical question is what happens when the model uses that key over a landmark—whether it retrieves the landmark's direct city or the actual capital. ady done country-of internally — which is the composition we're testing. So h-alone should NOT land the capital. This is genuinely shortcut-free.

For the implementation, I'll extend fn_stack.py with a new --chain flag to support the continent→capital chain rather than creating a separate instrument. This adds a COUNTRY_ mapping to the existing setup.

Now I'm laying out the design for the pre-reg specification, keeping just the deltas from the inherited gates and verdicts, then asking for freeze approval before building.

Confirmed against ground truth. Filtering to landmarks whose **city ≠ country's capital** gives a clean shortcut-free set:

**10 shortcut-free landmarks** (8 countries): Sagrada Família→Spain→**Madrid** (city Barcelona), Taj Mahal→India→**New Delhi** (Agra), Kaaba→Saudi Arabia→**Riyadh** (Mecca), Angkor Wat→Cambodia→**Phnom Penh** (Siem Reap), Burj Khalifa→UAE→**Abu Dhabi** (Dubai), Pyramids/Sphinx→Egypt→**Cairo** (Giza), Karnak→Egypt→**Cairo** (Luxor), Medina→Morocco→**Rabat** (Marrakech), Victoria Falls→Zambia→**Lusaka** (Livingstone). *(Excluded: Rome/Paris/Athens/Moscow/Berlin/KL/Beijing landmarks where city = capital; South Africa for its multi-capital ambiguity.)*

## §P-STACK-1b — shortcut-free composition (PRE-REG DRAFT — freeze on GO)

Inherits **all gates (G1/G2/G3), all 8 arms, all 4 window-pairs, and all verdicts** from §P-STACK-1 verbatim. Only the chain and readout change:

- **Chain:** landmark →(country-of)→ country →(**country→capital**)→ capital. Composed truth = `CAP_OF[COUNTRY_OF[landmark]]`. The capital is **not a direct attribute of the landmark** (its city is a *non-capital* city), so it is genuinely 2-hop-only.
- **h = country→capital** (NEW ground-truth map `COUNTRY_CAP`; key from held-out exemplars Portugal→Lisbon, Japan→Tokyo, Kenya→Nairobi — the same held-out countries mh3 already uses).
- **Readout union** adds the 8 capitals + keeps the direct cities → **three distinguishable outcomes:** composed = **capital** ✓; stopped-at-g = **country**; **shortcut = the landmark's direct city** (the confound, now a *separate* token from the answer).
- **The clean discriminator:** h-alone (country→capital key over a landmark) can at most produce the **direct city** (shortcut) — it *cannot* produce the capital without first doing country-of. So **only the stack can land the capital.** This is exactly what P-STACK-1 lacked: the composed answer can now *win the argmax* because it isn't competing with a 1-hop edge.
- **New advisory diagnostic:** `landed_on_city` fraction (shortcut rate) alongside `stopped_at_g`.

**Instrument:** extend `fn_stack.py` with a `--chain {continent,capital}` flag (open slot, addition — λ extend/one_way, no fork); `continent` reproduces the frozen P-STACK-1 exactly. **Honest scope:** n=10 cells (smaller than P-STACK-1's 18 — paired permutation over 10; the composition-window effect was p=1e-4 at 18, should survive but flagged); multi-token capitals graded on first token (New/Phnom/Abu — collisions dropped). Host order: `--validate` (add a `landed_on_city` planted world) → 4B smoke → 32B verdict, tmux main:1.

**Prediction (a-priori):** if the P-STACK-1 signal was genuine typed composition (not the shortcut), the shortcut-free chain should now show the composed **capital winning the argmax** (real accuracy, not just less-negative margin) at the composition window, with h-alone landing the **city** not the capital.

---

This is the measurement that turns P-STACK-1's marginal-in-the-margins result into a clean win-or-lose. Freeze **§P-STACK-1b** and build the `--chain capital` extension + `COUNTRY_CAP` map? Or adjust the design first (e.g., you'd rather widen n by relaxing the South-Africa/multi-capital exclusion, or pick a different non-transitive relation than capital)?

def main() -> int:
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="P-STACK-1 in-context composition")
    ap.add_argument("--validate", action="store_true")
    ap.add_argument("--model-id", default="Qwen/Qwen3-4B")
    ap.add_argument("--device", default="mps")
    ap.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16", choices=["float32", "bfloat16"])
    ap.add_argument("--ref-layer", type=int, default=9)
    ap.add_argument("--scale", type=float, default=2.0)
    ap.add_argument("--key-scale", type=float, default=2.0)
    ap.add_argument("--n-cells", type=int, default=0,
                    help="cap landmarks (0 = all valid; smoke uses ~8)")
    ap.add_argument("--alpha", type=float, default=0.05)
    ap.add_argument("--seed", type=int, default=0)
    ap.add_argument("--out", default="results/fn-stack/qwen3-4b")
    args = ap.parse_args()
    if args.validate:
        return run_validate(args.alpha)
    return run_model(args)

### Thinking

The stacking approach doesn't improve performance—the best individual model achieves 30% accuracy while stacking only reaches 20%, and across all tested pairs, stacking consistently underperforms, so it's not a viable strategy here.

Looking at specific examples, I notice composition works on some landmarks like Taj Mahal (where stacking correctly identifies New Delhi while the single model fails) and Angkor, Burj, and Sphinx, but fails unpredictably on others like Sagrada Familia and the Pyramids. The single model also partially succeeds at the task, reaching 20-30% accuracy on its own, so the issue isn't that composition is fundamentally impossible—it's just unreliable across the board.

What's striking is that certain cities like Paris and Agra keep appearing as outputs across different landmarks, suggesting they're acting as strong attractors in the model's representation space, pulling predictions toward them regardless of the actual landmark-capital relationship.

The real takeaway here is that the stacking approach doesn't reliably outperform its individual components on this capital task. The composition succeeds on maybe 4 out of 10 test cases, but h-alone also lands correctly on about 3 out of 10, so there's no clear win for the stacked approach. The injected key method for the capital chain seems too noisy and weak to demonstrate genuine compositional reasoning at this scale.

This suggests the earlier positive result on P-STACK-1 for continents may have been inflated by shortcuts — when I remove the single-hop workaround and require the answer to actually win through composition, the two-key stacking doesn't reliably work. Though there's still a confound here: h-alone still manages to land the capital 20-30% of the time because the country-to-capital key can still trigger internally even without explicit stacking, and the small sample size of 10 makes it hard to draw firm conclusions.

There is a weak positive trend in the data — stacking outperforms the best single approach by about 0.6 margin with p=0.06 — but it doesn't reach significance and the flip test fails. Order sensitivity is strong though, which suggests the ordered injection is doing something, just not producing reliable compositional wins.

The honest takeaway is that in-context assembly of programs from injected keys is fundamentally weak. The mechanism exists and shows order-sensitivity, but it doesn't reliably win when the answer isn't directly reachable in a single hop. This points back to weight-baking as the more reliable path forward. ly at P-BAKE-STACK as the necessary next step. I need to draft the results section for the 32B model with memory and present it to Michael. I'll commit the raw results first, then prepare the synthesis batch for review. The honest framing here is that in-context two-key stacking is fundamentally weak — the mechanism exists and shows order sensitivity and typing, but it doesn't reliably outperform, which means weight-baking is the required path forward.

But I need to be scientifically rigorous about something critical: my pre-registration explicitly predicted that removing shortcuts would reveal P-STACK-1 as inflated, and that's exactly what happened. The shortcut-free control came back NOT-STACKABLE, which means the TYPED-STACKABLE verdict I got before was at least partially margin-driven. The mechanism signatures are real, but the claim needs to be downgraded — it's weak and didn't survive the control. This is a significant tempering of the rung-2 finding, and I have to report it honestly rather than soften it.

So the actual state is that in-context stacking isn't reliably established — the mechanism exists but is fragile and collapsed under the shortcut-free test. That makes P-BAKE-STACK the more defensible path forward, since in-context alone isn't holding up. I'm going to draft the results with appropriate conservatism and commit to this reading.

### Thinking

The shortcut-free control came back as not stackable, which confirms my pre-registered hypothesis that the P-STACK-1 positive result was at least partially driven by shortcuts or margin inflation—this weakens the second tier of findings. I need to draft the revised result section accounting for this, add a note about the memory implications, and prepare everything for review.

Looking at the honest synthesis across the data: P-STACK-1b shows composition fails reliably when answers aren't single-hop reachable and require winning the argmax—the in-context two-key stacking doesn't compose consistently (g1 is nonsignificant, flips fail, stack accuracy of 0.20 versus h-alone at 0.30). There are isolated wins like Taj Mahal→New Delhi where stacking succeeds where h-alone fails, but this doesn't generalize across the n=10 cells with strong attractors, where h-alone retains 20-30% of the shortcut. Order sensitivity is genuine and substantial—the ordered injection shifts performance by 2.7 to 3.4 points, though wrong window placement kills it entirely—but this mechanism doesn't reliably produce composition. Combined with P-STACK-1's marginal continent positive sitting below the floor, the honest reading is that in-context assembly is fundamentally weak: the mechanism exists and is order-sensitive, but it doesn't reliably win against the baseline.

This means P-STACK-1's TYPED-STACKABLE claim was partly inflated by shortcut and margin effects, so I'm downgrading that to "weak and unreliable in-context; mechanism present but insufficient." The implication is clear: reliable programs need weight-baking, which makes P-BAKE-STACK the necessary next rung. This is a strong scientific outcome—the control tempered the claim exactly as it should, and it sharpens the artifact thesis: programs must be baked into weights, not assembled in-context.

I'm drafting the result synthesis and memory note, then presenting this for approval and offering Michael the choice between moving to P-BAKE-STACK or closing out this session, since we've answered the in-context stacking question decisively.

## Assistant

Committed (`323c743`). Here's the honest read — and it matters, because this was a control on my own prior claim.

## P-STACK-1b (shortcut-free) 32B — scored

**Frozen verdict: NOT-STACKABLE.** At the best pair (L19→L38): G1 stack−best-single = +0.605, **p=0.062 (n.s.** at α/4=0.0125), flip fails (**stack acc 0.20 ≤ h-alone 0.30**). No pair passes. Ceilings 10/10, gate-0 clean.

**What actually happened, per cell (best pair):**
- **Genuine composition on some cells:** Taj Mahal→**New Delhi** (stack lands it, h-alone lands Paris — the stack *composed* where the single key failed); Angkor→Phnom Penh, Burj→Abu Dhabi, Sphinx→Cairo.
- **Failures/noise:** Sagrada→stack lands **Barcelona** (the city shortcut); Kaaba/Pyramids→Paris; Karnak/Medina→Agra (strong union attractors).
- **h-alone still partially reaches the capital (20–30%)** — the country→capital key over a landmark sometimes triggers the internal 2-hop anyway, so even this chain isn't a *perfectly* clean shortcut kill.

**The honest interpretation — this tempers rung 2.** My a-priori pre-registration said explicitly: *null ⟹ the P-STACK-1 positive was shortcut/margin-inflated.* The shortcut-free control came back **NOT-STACKABLE**. So intellectual honesty requires downgrading the P-STACK-1 "TYPED-STACKABLE" claim:

> **In-context assembly of programs from injected keys is WEAK.** The *mechanism* is real — order-sensitivity is strong and robust (order +2.7→+3.4, wrong-window dead) and types grade in the margins — but when you remove the single-hop shortcut *and* require the composed answer to actually **win the argmax**, two-key stacking does **not reliably compose** (g1 n.s., stack ≤ h-alone). P-STACK-1's positive lived in sub-floor margins against a reachable target; it did not survive its own control.

**This is a clean, valuable outcome** (and exactly why the control was worth running before baking): it says **the seam exists but is not reliable in-context → programs must be baked into weights to be robust.** That points straight at **P-BAKE-STACK** (the original rung 3) as the *necessary* path, not an optional one. Caveats kept honest: n=10, strong attractors (Paris/Agra), residual partial shortcut in h-alone.

## Assistant

s293 closed and committed (`380328e`). Here's what we learned.

## What we learned this session (s293)

### 1. The theory seed is genuinely generative — but it inverts the sign on capacity/scale
Oracle round 1 handed a cold agent *only* the compressed theory and asked it to predict four sealed 32B verdicts it had never seen. Score +2 (6/10) — but the **distribution** was the finding, not the number:
- **It derived, not echoed, the hard ones.** It got both XTERM sub-verdicts ("storage is linear, interference happens in the light") purely by unfolding the seed's `write ≡ inject` + `retrieve ≡ illuminate` split. That's a theory acting as a real prior.
- **It inverted the sign on every capacity/scale question.** All 4 misses were directional; 3 were the CAP family — it predicted crosstalk-*hurts* (reality: coherent-*gain*), decline (reality: rise), √(D/k) (reality: unexpressed). The seed carried a naïve HRR intuition. **We fixed the seed** (added a coherent-vs-destructive interference clause; demoted √(D/k)).
- **Lesson:** a compressed theory can be right about *mechanism* and wrong about the *sign of a magnitude* — and Oracle localizes that gap for the cost of one agent spawn, no GPU.

### 2. Function-composition from injected keys exists — but is *weak in-context*
This is the session's spine. We descended the program-plates ladder:
- **Rung 1 (established):** FN-INDEX already showed single-function content-addressable dispatch.
- **Rung 2 attempt — P-STACK-1:** two injected keys, compose h(g(X)). Frozen verdict *TYPED-STACKABLE*, but I flagged it as **marginal** — the effect lived in sub-floor negative margins (~6% absolute accuracy). The mechanism signatures were all present and clean: composition beats parts (p=1e-4), a **monotone type ladder** (well > near > far > random — the linker's `composable ⟺ product(g) ∈ key_passband(h)` made behavioral), and **order-sensitivity** (wrong-window dead — a real program counter).
- **The control — P-STACK-1b:** we removed the confound. Geography is *transitively closed* (a landmark's continent is directly knowable), so P-STACK-1's "composition" could ride a single-hop shortcut. We built a **shortcut-free chain** (landmark→country→**capital**, where the city ≠ capital, so the answer is genuinely 2-hop-only) and required the composed answer to actually **win the argmax**.
- **Result: NOT-STACKABLE.** Composition happened on *some* cells (Taj Mahal→New Delhi: the stack composed where the single key failed) but not reliably (stack acc 0.20 ≤ h-alone 0.30, g1 n.s.).

### 3. The control did its job — and honestly downgraded my own prior claim
I had **pre-registered** the falsifier: *if the shortcut-free chain comes back null, P-STACK-1's positive was shortcut-inflated.* It came back null. So intellectual honesty required tempering rung 2:

> **In-context assembly of programs from injected keys is WEAK.** The mechanism is real (order-sensitive, typed in the margins), but it does not reliably win the argmax once the answer isn't single-hop reachable. The continent-chain flip was largely the shortcut.

### 4. The scientific payoff: weight-baking is *necessary*, not optional
The two experiments together give a coherent conclusion that sharpens the artifact thesis: **the seam exists but can't be reliably assembled in-context → programs must be burned into weights.** That makes **P-BAKE-STACK** (the original rung 3) the load-bearing next step, not an afterthought.

### 5. Method lessons worth carrying forward
- **Run the shortcut-free control before believing a margin-based positive** — a transitively-closed KB inflates composition claims.
- **λ yardstick / λ measure held its ground twice:** a technically-passed gate (TYPED-STACKABLE on relative margins) was not allowed to manufacture a stronger claim than the data supported.
- **The 4B→32B pattern recurred (~5th time):** both chains were NOT-STACKABLE at 4B (it compresses/shortcuts); at 32B they *split* — continent flipped to marginal-positive (the shortcut), capital stayed null (no shortcut). The divergence itself proved which effect was the shortcut.

**Where it leaves us (s294 cold-start):** ladder = rung 1 ✓, rung 2 weak, **rung 3 P-BAKE-STACK now load-bearing**. Open alternatives on the books: Oracle round 2 (the seed now has the coherent-gain fix + two more verdicts postdating it), or the type-census / think / pythia-scratch fronts. Discipline note stands: close before opening.

The through-line: **we tried to program the model in-context from parts, found the mechanism is real but too weak to trust, and the failure told us exactly where reliability must come from — the weights.**

## User

Ok so when we inject without baking, it's making it so our injection only changes the probablity on hop 1 but not hop 2?


### Thinking

Michael's asking whether injection without baking only affects the first hop—changing what the model produces initially—but fails to propagate through the second hop since the injected key doesn't actually drive the downstream computation. I need to examine the runtime data carefully to test this mechanistic hypothesis rather than speculate about why in-context stacking underperforms.

Looking at the actual results: in the continent chain, g-alone correctly lands the country (hop 1 works), h-alone at the composition window is completely dead, and stacking only achieves 6% accuracy on continent with 22% stopping at the country output. For the capital chain, the stopped_at_g diagnostic shows zero across all pairs—nobody halts at the country—yet stacking lands capital only 20% while h-alone reaches 20-30%. The key diagnostic here is whether stopped_at_g captures evidence of the injection failing to propagate past the first hop.

The mechanics matter: g-key at w_g should compute g(X) = country in the residual, then h-key at w_h should read that country and apply h to get the continent. But when both keys inject at the same final token, h might not actually be reading g's output—it could be operating on the original input instead, which would explain why hop 2 fails to complete reliably. learly in the right form when h tries to read it—g's output might be noisy or positioned where h's attention can't cleanly extract it. So h's key-based selection fails to properly illuminate g's country representation, breaking the composition chain.

Looking at the empirical results, this explains why h works well when applied directly to the landmark (the original operand at that position) but struggles when it needs to compose with g's intermediate output. When h illuminates the landmark itself, it can follow its direct shortcuts—landmark to continent or landmark to city—which is why the standalone h performance was decent. But when the chain requires h to read g's country output and map it forward, the operand isn't in the right state for h's key to work effectively.

The fundamental constraint is that injecting a key selects which function fires, but doesn't rewire what operand that function receives. So g produces the country and leaves it in the residual, yet h has no built-in way to treat that country as its input—it defaults back to the original landmark operand instead. Baking would solve this by encoding the g-to-h handoff directly into the weights, creating a learned pathway for the composition.

Looking at the empirical signal, the 22% "stopped at g" fraction is actually the clearest evidence: a meaningful portion of cells execute hop 1 and halt entirely, never attempting hop 2. That's the smoking gun that hop 1 is moving while hop 2 fails to complete. The broader 72% landing elsewhere suggests the two-key injection is noisy and perturbative overall, but that stopped fraction cuts through the noise. There's also the order-sensitivity finding—when h fires early and g fires late, the result is different, which points to a dependency on sequencing. Without baking, h fires generically without being conditioned on g's actual output — it just adds diffuse "h-type output" mass rather than h applied to g(X). The evidence bears this out: in P-STACK-1b, h alone lands various capitals including wrong ones like Paris for Kaaba or Taj Mahal, showing it's producing a spread of h-outputs rather than the correct capital that would result from proper conditioning on g's result.

Looking at the stack versus h-alone performance, the stack barely outperforms h-alone, suggesting that even when g-key is explicitly present, h isn't reliably reading g's specific output. So to answer Michael more precisely: Hop 1 does move — about 22% of cells visibly execute g and land on the intermediate — but Hop 2 is where the failure occurs, and it's not because h fails to fire.

h does produce the right type of output (continent or capital), but it's not conditioned on what g actually produced. Instead, it adds generic probability mass for "h-output-type" rather than "h applied to g's specific result," landing the right type but wrong value (Paris instead of Riyadh) or re-applying to the original operand. The real missing piece is operand handoff — an injected key can select a function but can't rebind h's input to be "whatever g just produced." That's what weight-baking would fix by making the composed answer win through physical binding.

This mechanistic interpretation fits the diagnostics (stopped-at-g, right-type-wrong-value from h alone), though it's a hypothesis rather than a direct causal measurement. I can check it by seeing whether stack errors are right-type-wrong-value (conditioning failure) versus wrong-type (h not firing). Let me verify the "right type, wrong value" claim against the actual capital data I have.

Looking at the stack results, Paris landed as an answer—but Paris isn't in the COUNTRY_CAP test set even though it's a capital of France. Paris is in the CITIES set, so it's being classified as a city rather than a capital-label. Same with Agra. So the wrong answers are actually cities (the hop-1 domain), not wrong capitals, which complicates the "right type wrong value" story. - Victoria Falls→Lusaka | stack: New Delhi | h: Agra

The pattern's becoming clearer: errors cluster around cities like Paris and Agra rather than capitals, suggesting the model gets trapped in a city-type attractor instead of cleanly retrieving the wrong capital. The data breaks down into two patterns: some landmark-to-capital chains compose correctly (4 out of 10), while the rest collapse onto salient city names—either the shortcut domain like Barcelona or strong attractors like Paris and Agra. The continent chain shows 22% of errors landing on the country output, making Michael's hypothesis about injection affecting hop-1 but not hop-2 directionally plausible there, though the capital chain tells a murkier story where the attractor effect dominates.

The real issue is that hop-2 isn't actually conditioned on hop-1's specific output—the injected h-key just biases toward h's output type generically, so without proper operand handoff the model can't apply h to what g actually produced. Instead it defaults to whatever place-name feels most salient, and the composed answer only emerges when the model naturally completes the chain end-to-end.

The missing piece is operand rebinding—the handoff that would let g's output become h's input, which is exactly what baking would install by making that binding physical. I can verify this interpretation against the stopped-at-g data I already have, and the order-sensitivity in the wrong-window errors confirms the model is genuinely trying to sequence g-then-h, so the two-hop structure is actually operative.

Now I'm structuring a clear answer grounded in the specific numbers: the 22% stopped-at-g rate shows hop-1 fires and halts, the order-sensitivity proves sequencing matters, and the refinement is that h does fire but doesn't complete reliably—it's not that h has no effect, it's that the conditioning through h is lossy.

The core issue is operand rebinding: a key selects a function but can't pass g's output as h's input, so h just adds generic mass back to the original operand, collapsing onto salient place-names. The native completion path wins only about 40% of the time. This is exactly what the P-BAKE-STACK test is designed to isolate.

## Assistant

Yes — that's the right instinct, and the diagnostics point at it. But let me sharpen it one notch, because it's not quite that hop-2 *does nothing* — it's that **hop-2 fires but isn't conditioned on hop-1's output.**

## What the arms actually show

**Hop-1 does move.** In the continent chain, **22% of stacked cells "stop at g"** — they land on the intermediate *country* and halt. So the g-key writes its output (the country) into the residual; that part works. And **order-sensitivity is strong** (reverse the windows → dead) — the machinery genuinely tries to sequence g-then-h. So the structure is hop-1-then-hop-2, exactly as you'd expect.

**Hop-2 is where it breaks — but not by staying silent.** Look at where the *errors* land in the capital chain:

| landmark | wanted | stack landed |
|---|---|---|
| Taj Mahal | New Delhi | New Delhi ✓ |
| Angkor / Burj / Sphinx | (capital) | ✓ |
| Sagrada | Madrid | **Barcelona** (its own city) |
| Kaaba / Pyramids | Riyadh / Cairo | **Paris** |
| Karnak / Medina | Cairo / Rabat | **Agra** |

The failures don't land on *nothing* — they collapse onto salient place-names: the landmark's **own direct city** (the shortcut) or strong attractors (Paris, Agra). The h-key *is* firing and biasing the output toward "a place / an h-type answer" — it just isn't applying h to **the specific thing g produced**. It adds generic h-output mass, not h(g(X)) mass. That's why the *right* answer only wins on the ~4/10 cells where the model happens to complete the chain natively anyway.

## The precise missing ingredient: **operand rebinding**

An injected key can *select a function* — that's what FN-INDEX proved (dispatch works). What it **cannot** do is **rebind the operand** — make "whatever g just wrote" become h's input. So:

- g runs, writes the country into the residual.
- h runs, but its operand is still ambiguously the *original* installed landmark (→ the direct-city shortcut) or a generic "place," not g's country.
- The `product(g) ∈ key_passband(h)` handoff — the linker edge — **isn't there**. Nothing wires g's output register to h's input passband.

So your framing is basically right, refined to: *the injection reliably drives hop-1, and h fires, but the **g→h operand handoff** is missing — h isn't conditioned on hop-1's product, so it can't specialize to h(g(X)).*

## Why this is the exact case for baking

That handoff is a *structural* connection between two functions. In-context injection is a per-token additive nudge — it has no way to install a persistent "route g's output into h's operand slot" wire. **Weight-baking is precisely the operation that installs that wire** (the linker edge made physical). 

So P-BAKE-STACK isn't just "make it stronger" — it's testing the specific mechanistic prediction this session produced: **does burning the stack to a delta plate install the operand handoff, so hop-2 becomes conditioned on hop-1's output and the composed answer wins where in-context injection couldn't?**

One honest caveat: this is an *interpretation* grounded in the stopped-at-g fraction + the where-errors-land pattern, not yet a direct causal measurement. It's cheaply checkable — e.g., a diagnostic on whether stack errors concentrate in the hop-1/operand domain (cities) vs the h-output domain (wrong capitals) would confirm "conditioning failure" vs "h-not-firing." Worth a probe when we pick this up.
